Adding a constant and an axiom to a doctrine
Francesca Guffanti

Abstract
We study the meaning of “adding a constant to a language” for any doctrine, and “adding an axiom to a theory” for a primary doctrine, by showing how these are actually two instances of the same construction. We prove their universal properties, and how these constructions are compatible with additional structure on the doctrine. Existence of Kleisli object for comonads in the 2-category of indexed poset is proved in order to build these constructions.



1
Introduction

Given a theory
\mathcal{T}
in a first-order language
\mathcal{L}
, consider for each set of variables the set of well-formed formulae written with at most those variables. That set can be ordered by provable consequence in the theory
\mathcal{T}
. By that we mean that the formula
\alpha
is less than or equal to the formula
\beta
if the consequence
\alpha\vdash_{\mathcal{T}}\beta
holds. The logical operations of conjunction, disjunction, implication, negation, true and false give this set the structure of a Boolean algebra. And the assignment
\vec{x}\mapsto\mathrm{WFF}(\vec{x})
of the Boolean algebra of formulae to a list (i.e. a context) of distinct variables can be extended to a functor
\mathrm{WFF}:\mathrm{\mathbb{C}tx}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{BA}
from the opposite category of contexts and terms to the category of Boolean algebras and homomorphisms.

This can be considered the motivating example at the basis of the notion of hyperdoctrine which was introduced by Lawvere in 1969 in a series of seminal papers
[
Law69a
,
Law69b
,
Law70
]
. It is a categorical tool that allows the analysis of both syntax and semantics of logical theories through the same mathematical structure. One of the main intuitions of Lawvere was to recognize that quantifiers in logic are instances of adjunctions between the posets of formulae.

A doctrine is possibly the basic fabric of Lawvere’s hyperdoctrine: just a functor
P:\mathbb{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
, from a category
\mathbb{C}
with finite products into the category
\mathbf{Pos}
of partially ordered sets and monotone functions.

In this framework, a possible direction is to understand what classical results in logic can be interpreted in the language of doctrines, and how to do so. In first-order model theory it is not uncommon for proofs to rely on adding constant symbols to a language and axioms to a theory. For example, the proof of Henkin’s Theorem
[
Hen49
]
—
every consistent first-order theory has a model
— is done by at first extending the language with new constant symbols, and then adding new axioms of the form
\exists x\varphi(x)\to\varphi(c)
to the theory. Henkin’s Theorem is now the standard way to prove one of the most crucial results in first-order logic: Gödel’s Completeness Theorem
[
Göd30
]
—
if a closed formula is valid in every first-order structure, then there is a first-order proof of it
. A similar process can be used in the proof of the Upward Löwenheim-Skolem-Tarski Theorem
[
CK90
]
—
a theory which has an infinite model has a model in every cardinality
\kappa\geq\max(|\mathcal{L}|,\aleph_{0})
: after having extended the language
\mathcal{L}
with a suitable amount of constant symbols, axioms of the form
\lnot(c=d)
are added to the theory. Many other examples arise in algebra: the theory of pointed sets is obtained from the theory of sets by adding one constant symbol, the theory of abelian groups is obtained from the theory of groups by adding the commutativity axiom, the theory of monoids is obtained from the theory of semigroups by adding a constant
e
and the axiom
\forall x((x\cdot e=x)\land(e\cdot x=x))
, and so on.

Thus, translating the concepts of adding constants and axioms to a theory is a tool which could be relevant for future applications, with the aim to interpret results of first-order logic in the doctrinal setting. However, before understanding what it means to add
any
amount of constants and axioms to a doctrine, it is reasonable to start this investigation in the finitary case, which is the focus of this work.

In more detail, we extend to doctrines the construction of adding a constant to a language.
And we also extend the construction of forcing a new axiom for primary doctrines, which are doctrines where all orders are inf-semilattices and reindexing preserves them—essentially, what it amounts to the ability to interpret conjunctions of formulae.
Actually, we do both constructions in one step, using a Kleisli object for a convenient comonad on the original doctrine seen as an indexed poset. The existence of Kleisli objects for comonads in indexed posets is proven in Proposition
2.19
.
Let
P:\mathbb{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
be a primary doctrine, let
X
be a fixed object, and let
\varphi\in P(X)
be an element. In keeping a reasonable parallel with the logical intuition, think of the primary doctrine
P
as the syntactic consequences of a theory—not just formulae of a language, but that is already a good intuition—; think of an object
X
as a list of fresh variables, and think of
\varphi
as a formula in the fresh variables. We construct a primary doctrine
P_{(X,\varphi)}
and a homomorphism of primary doctrines
P\to P_{(X,\varphi)}
. Staying with the parallel, the doctrine
P_{(X,\varphi)}
acts like the extension of the theory with new constant symbols and with the new axiom
\varphi
evaluated in those constants. The homomorphism of doctrines acts like a translation of the original theory into the new one.

The construction has a universal property: any other morphism of primary doctrines
P\to R
such that the interpretation of
\varphi
evaluated in some constant in
R
is true factors through
P\to P_{(X,\varphi)}
, essentially in a unique way. This is, in broad terms, the statement of Theorem
6.2
. Moreover, the result is extended to 2-arrows in Theorem
6.4
. We also show throughout
Section

5
that the construction
P\to P_{(X,\varphi)}
preserves many additional structures and properties that the original doctrine may already enjoy.

The construction includes the two constructions we discussed at the
beginning: adding no axiom has a structural parallel in adding
\top

to the axioms, while adding no constant corresponds to performing the
construction picking the terminal object for
X
.
We can clearly decide to add just a constant of sort
X
,
without adding any axiom to the theory, and obtain a homomorphism
P\to P_{X}
.
Similarly, we can decide to add just an axiom
\varphi
to a
theory, without adding any constant symbol, and obtain the homomorphism

P\to P_{\varphi}
.

2
Preliminaries

In this section, we lay the groundwork for the paper by introducing the language of doctrines and establishing their key properties. We then compute the Kleisli objects for comonads in the 2-category of indexed posets, which will be the basis for our main construction. Most of the notions and results concerning category theory used in this thesis are standard, and we refer to any textbook, for instance
[
Bor94a
,
Joh02
,
Mac71
]
.

2.1
Doctrines

In this subsection, we define the 2-category of doctrines and show some relevant examples. Then we will gradually add more structure in order to be able to interpret symbols of first-order logic—such as connectives and quantifiers—in the context of doctrines.

Definition 2.1
.

Let
\mathbb{C}
be a category with finite products and let
\mathbf{Pos}
be the category of partially-ordered sets and monotone functions. A
doctrine
is a functor
P:\mathbb{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
. The category
\mathbb{C}
is called
base category of
P
, each poset
P(X)
for an object
X\in\mathbb{C}
is called
fiber
, the function
P(f)
for an arrow
f
in
\mathbb{C}
is called
reindexing
.

By viewing doctrines as a broad generalization of doctrines of well-formed formulae, we can interpret the objects of category
\mathbb{C}
as lists of variables, the arrows as terms, the fibers as sets of formulae, and reindexing as substitutions, providing an intuitive understanding of the structure.

Example 2.2
.

We propose the following examples.

(a)

The functor
\mathscr{P}:\textnormal{Set}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
, sending each set in the poset of its subsets, ordered by inclusion, and each function
f:A\to B
to the inverse image
f^{-1}:\mathscr{P}(B)\to\mathscr{P}(A)
is a doctrine.

(b)

For a given category
\mathbb{C}
with finite limits, the functor
\mathop{\mathrm{Sub}}_{\mathbb{C}}:\mathbb{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
sending each object to the poset of its subobjects in
\mathbb{C}
and each arrow
f:A\to B
to the pullback function
f^{*}:\mathop{\mathrm{Sub}}_{\mathbb{C}}(B)\to\mathop{\mathrm{Sub}}_{\mathbb{C}}(A)
, is a doctrine.

(c)

For a given theory
\mathcal{T}
on a one-sorted first-order language
\mathcal{L}
, define the category
\mathrm{\mathbb{C}tx}_{\mathcal{L}}
of contexts: an object is a finite list of distinct variables and an arrow between two lists
\vec{x}=(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
and
\vec{y}=(y_{1},\dots,y_{m})
is

(t_{1}(\vec{x}),\dots,t_{m}(\vec{x})):(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\to(y_{1},\dots,y_{m})

an
m
-tuple of terms in the context
\vec{x}
. The empty list
()
is the terminal object in
\mathrm{\mathbb{C}tx}_{\mathcal{L}}
, the product of two lists
\vec{x}
and
\vec{y}
in
\mathrm{\mathbb{C}tx}_{\mathcal{L}}
is given by any list whose length is the sum of the length of
\vec{x}
and
\vec{y}
—if the variables in the two lists are all distinct, their product can be written as the juxtaposition
\langle\vec{x};\vec{y}\rangle=(x_{1}\dots,x_{n},y_{1},\dots,y_{m})
. The functor
\mathtt{LT}^{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathcal{T}}:\mathrm{\mathbb{C}tx}_{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
sends each list of variables
\vec{x}
to the Lindenbaum-Tarski algebra of well-formed formulae with free variables in
\vec{x}
modulo the theory
\mathcal{T}
, meaning that
\mathtt{LT}^{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathcal{T}}(\vec{x})
is the poset reflection of well-formed formulae written with free variables in
\vec{x}
, possibly dummy, ordered by provable consequence in
\mathcal{T}
. Moreover,
\mathtt{LT}^{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathcal{T}}:\mathrm{\mathbb{C}tx}_{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
sends an arrow
\vec{t}(\vec{x}):\vec{x}\to\vec{y}
into the substitution
[\vec{t}(\vec{x})/\vec{y}]
, that maps the equivalence class of a formula
\alpha(\vec{y})
to the equivalence class of the formula
\alpha(\vec{t}(\vec{x})/\vec{y})
. We refer to any standard textbook about first-order logic for definitions of concepts including language, variables, theory, terms, substitution, formulae, see for instance
[
TZ12
]
.

(d)

For a given category
\mathbb{D}
with finite products and weak pullbacks, the functor of weak subobjects
\mathbf{\Psi}_{\mathbb{D}}:\mathbb{D}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
sending each object
A
to the poset reflection of the comma category
\mathbb{D}/A
is a doctrine: for each arrow
f:A\to B
,
\mathbf{\Psi}_{\mathbb{D}}(f)
sends the equivalence class of an arrow
\alpha:\mathop{\mathrm{dom}}\alpha\to B
to the equivalence class of the projection
\pi_{1}
of a chosen weak pullback of
\alpha
along
f
—see Example 2.9 in
[
MR13b
]
for more details.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathop{\mathrm{dom}}\alpha}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

Definition 2.3
.

A
doctrine homomorphism
—1-cell or 1-arrow—between
P:\mathbb{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
and
R:\mathbb{D}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
is a pair
(F,\mathfrak{f})
where
F:\mathbb{C}\to\mathbb{D}
is a functor that preserves finite products and
\mathfrak{f}:P\xrightarrow{\cdot}R\circ F^{\mathrm{op}}
is a natural transformation. Sometimes a morphism between
P
and
R
will be called a model of
P
in
R
.
A
2-cell
between
(F,\mathfrak{f})
and
(G,\mathfrak{g})
from
P
to
R
is a natural transformation
\theta:F\xrightarrow{\cdot}G
such that
\mathfrak{f}_{A}(\alpha)\leq R(\theta_{A})(\mathfrak{g}_{A}(\alpha))
for any object
A
in
\mathbb{C}
and
\alpha\in P(A)
. Doctrine, doctrine morphisms with 2-cells defined here form a
2
-category, that will be denoted
\mathbf{Dct}
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{C}^{\mathrm{op}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{D}^{\mathrm{op}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{C}^{\mathrm{op}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{D}^{\mathrm{op}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{Pos}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{Pos}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{\mathrm{op}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{\mathrm{op}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G^{\mathrm{op}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{g}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta^{\mathrm{op}}}

By definition of doctrine, the fibers are simply posets. However, we can define specific doctrines by imposing additional structure on these posets or by requiring the existence of adjoints to certain reindexing. To work in a setting that interprets the conjunction of formulae and the true constant, primary doctrines are necessary.

Definition 2.4
.

A
primary doctrine

P:\mathbb{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
is a doctrine such that for each object
A
in
\mathbb{C}
, the poset
P(A)
has finite meets, and the related operations
\land:P\times P\xrightarrow{\cdot}P
and
\top:\mathbf{1}\xrightarrow{\cdot}P
yield natural transformations.

Example 2.5
.

Examples seen in
2.2
are primary doctrines:

(a)

For any set
A
, intersection of two subsets is their meet,
A
is the top element.

(b)

For any object
A
in
\mathbb{C}
, the pullback of a subobject along another defines their meet.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathop{\mathrm{dom}}(\alpha\land\beta)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathop{\mathrm{dom}}\alpha}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathop{\mathrm{dom}}\beta}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha\land\beta}

The arrow
\OPRid_{A}
is the top element.

(c)

For any list
\vec{x}
, the conjunction of two formulae is their binary meet, the true constant
\top
is the top element.

(d)

For any object
A
in
\mathbb{D}
, a choice of a weak pullback of a representative of a weak subobject along another defines their meet

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}},

the class of
\OPRid_{A}
is the top element.

In order to interpret equality, we recall the notion of elementary doctrines. The definition we provide is not the classical one given by Lawvere in
[
Law70
]
, but it is the equivalent version that can be found in Proposition 2.5 of
[
EPR20
]
:

Definition 2.6
.

A primary doctrine
P:\mathbb{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
is
elementary
if for any object
A
in
\mathbb{C}
there exists an element
\delta_{A}\in P(A\times A)
such that:

1.

\top_{A}\leq P(\Delta_{A})(\delta_{A})
;

2.

P(A)=\mathscr{D}es_{\delta_{A}}:=\{\alpha\in P(A)\mid P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\alpha)\land\delta_{A}\leq P(\mathrm{pr}_{2})(\alpha)\}
;

3.

\delta_{A}\boxtimes\delta_{B}\leq\delta_{A\times B}
, where
\delta_{A}\boxtimes\delta_{B}=P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{3}\rangle)(\delta_{A})\land P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{2},\mathrm{pr}_{4}\rangle)(\delta_{B})
.

In 2.,
\mathrm{pr}_{1}
and
\mathrm{pr}_{2}
are the projections from
A\times A
in
A
; in 3., the projections are from
A\times B\times A\times B
. The element
\delta_{A}
will be called
fibered equality
on
A
.

Example 2.7
.

Examples seen in Example
2.2
are elementary doctrines:

(a)

For any set
A
, the subset
\Delta_{A}\subseteq A\times A
is the fibered equality on
A
.

(b)

For any object
A
in
\mathbb{C}
, the map
\Delta_{A}:A\rightarrowtail A\times A
is the fibered equality on
A
—see in
[
MR13a
]
the Example 2.4.a.

(c)

Provided that the language
\mathcal{L}
has equality, for any list
\vec{x}
, the formula
\big(x_{1}=x_{1}^{\prime}\land\dots\land x_{n}=x_{n}^{\prime}\big)
in
\mathtt{LT}^{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathcal{T}}(\vec{x};\vec{x}^{\prime})
is the fibered equality on
\vec{x}
.

(d)

For any object
A
in
\mathbb{D}
, the equivalence class of the map
\Delta_{A}:A\rightarrowtail A\times A
is the fibered equality on
A
.

We now generalize the existential and universal quantifiers, which are defined as adjoint to some reindexing.

Definition 2.8
.

A primary doctrine
P:\mathbb{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
is
existential
if for any pair of objects
B,C
of
\mathbb{C}
, the map
P(\mathrm{pr}_{1}):P(C)\to P(C\times B)
has a left adjoint

\exists^{B}_{C}:P(C\times B)\to P(C),

satisfying:

-

Beck-Chevalley condition with respect to pullback diagrams of the form:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C\times B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C^{\prime}\times B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\times\OPRid_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{pr}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{pr}_{1}}

that is,
\exists^{B}_{C}P(f\times\OPRid_{B})=P(f)\exists^{B}_{C^{\prime}}
.

-

Frobenius reciprocity, that is, for any
\alpha\in P(C\times B)
and
\beta\in P(C)
it holds
\exists^{B}_{C}(\alpha\land P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\beta))=\exists^{B}_{C}(\alpha)\land\beta
.

Definition 2.9
.

A doctrine
P:\mathbb{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
is
universal
if for any pair of objects
B,C
of
\mathbb{C}
, the map
P(\mathrm{pr}_{1}):P(C)\to P(C\times B)
has a right adjoint
\forall^{B}_{C}:P(C\times B)\to P(C)
, satisfying Beck-Chevalley condition with respect to pullback diagrams of the same form as Definition
2.8
, that is
P(f)\forall^{B}_{C^{\prime}}=\forall^{B}_{C}P(f\times\OPRid_{B})
.

Definition 2.10
.

A doctrine
P:\mathbb{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
:

•

is
implicational
if for any object
A
, the poset
P(A)
is cartesian closed, and the related operations
\land:P\times P\xrightarrow{\cdot}P
,
\top:\mathbf{1}\xrightarrow{\cdot}P
,
\to:P^{\mathrm{op}}\times P\xrightarrow{\cdot}P
yield natural transformations–in particular it is a primary doctrine;

•

has bottom element
if for any object
A
, the poset
P(A)
has a bottom element, and the related operation,
\bot:\mathbf{1}\xrightarrow{\cdot}P
yields a natural transformation;

•

is bounded
if for any object
A
, the poset
P(A)
has a top and a bottom element, and the related operation,
\top:\mathbf{1}\xrightarrow{\cdot}P
and
\bot:\mathbf{1}\xrightarrow{\cdot}P
yield natural transformations;

•

has finite joins
if for any object
A
, the poset
P(A)
has finite joins, and the related operations
\lor:P\times P\xrightarrow{\cdot}P
,
\bot:\mathbf{1}\xrightarrow{\cdot}P
yield natural transformations;

•

is
Heyting
if for any object
A
, the poset
P(A)
is an Heyting algebra, and the related operations
\land:P\times P\xrightarrow{\cdot}P
,
\top:\mathbf{1}\xrightarrow{\cdot}P
,
\to:P^{\mathrm{op}}\times P\xrightarrow{\cdot}P
,
\lor:P\times P\xrightarrow{\cdot}P
,
\bot:\mathbf{1}\xrightarrow{\cdot}P
yield natural transformations;

•

is
Boolean
if it is Heyting and the operation
\lnot(\mathrm{-}):=(\mathrm{-})\to\bot:P^{\mathrm{op}}\xrightarrow{\cdot}P
is an isomorphism.

Example 2.11
.

The doctrine
\mathtt{LT}^{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathcal{T}}:\mathrm{\mathbb{C}tx}_{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
is Boolean elementary existential universal: in addition to the structure mentioned in the examples above, the implication of two formulae gives the implicational structure, the disjunction of two formulae is their join, the false is the bottom element, existential and universal quantifier define the left and the right adjoint to the inclusions of formulae
\mathtt{LT}^{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathcal{T}}(\vec{x})\subseteq\mathtt{LT}^{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathcal{T}}(\vec{x};\vec{y})
for any pair
\vec{x}=(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
,
\vec{y}=(y_{1},\dots,y_{m})
:

Definition 2.12
.

Any homomorphism
(F,\mathfrak{f}):P\to R
from
P:\mathbb{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
to
R:\mathbb{D}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
is called respectively
primary, elementary, existential, universal, implicational, bounded, Heyting, Boolean
if both
P
and
R
are, and
\mathfrak{f}
preserves the said structure.
For example, an elementary homomorphism is such that for any object
A
in
\mathbb{C}
, and any
\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}\in P(A)
:

\mathfrak{f}_{A}(\alpha\land_{A}\alpha^{\prime})=\mathfrak{f}_{A}(\alpha)\land_{FA}\mathfrak{f}(\alpha^{\prime});\qquad\qquad\mathfrak{f}_{A}(\top_{A})=\top_{FA};\qquad\qquad\mathfrak{f}_{A\times A}(\delta_{A})=\delta_{FA};

while a universal homomorphism is such that for any pair of objects
B,C
in
\mathbb{C}
, and any element
\alpha\in P(C\times B)
:

\mathfrak{f}_{C}\forall^{B}_{C}(\alpha)=\forall^{FB}_{FC}\mathfrak{f}_{C\times B}(\alpha).

Notation 2.13
.

We will write
\mathbf{PD}
for the 2-full 2-subcategory of
\mathbf{Dct}
of primary doctrines and primary homomorphisms.

Example 2.14
.

For a given category
\mathbb{C}
with finite limits, the inclusion of
\mathop{\mathrm{Sub}}_{\mathbb{C}}(A)
into the poset reflection of
\mathbb{C}/A
yields a natural transformation
\mathop{\mathrm{Sub}}_{\mathbb{C}}\to\mathbf{\Psi}_{\mathbb{C}}
; pairing it with the identity on the base category
\mathbb{C}
, this defines an elementary homomorphism.

2.2
Kleisli constructions in the 2-category of indexed posets

The remaining part of the preliminaries section is devoted to showing the existence of Kleisli objects for comonads in the 2-category of indexed posets—that are essentially doctrines without the assumption of having a base category with finite products. We will use the Kleisi construction to define a new doctrine
P_{(X,\varphi)}
from a starting doctrine
P
in Section
4
and to prove its universal property in Section
6
. Before we delve into the details, we will provide a brief overview of the relevant definitions and concepts. In the 2-category
\mathbf{IdxPos}
of indexed posets the cells are defined as follows:

•

a
0-cell
is a functor
P:\mathbb{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
;

•

a
1-cell
between
P:\mathbb{C}^{\text{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
and
R:\mathbb{D}^{\text{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
is a pair
(F,\mathfrak{f})
where
F:\mathbb{C}\to\mathbb{D}
is a functor and
\mathfrak{f}:P\xrightarrow{\cdot}R\circ F^{\text{op}}
is a natural transformation;

•

a
2-cell
between
(F,\mathfrak{f}),(G,\mathfrak{g}):P\to R
is a natural transformation
\theta:F\xrightarrow{\cdot}G
such that
\mathfrak{f}_{A}(\alpha)\leq R(\theta_{A})(\mathfrak{g}_{A}(\alpha))
for any object
A
in
\mathbb{C}
and
\alpha\in P(A)
.

Remark 2.15
.

The 2-category
\mathbf{Dct}
is a 2-full 2-subcategory of
\mathbf{IdxPos}
.

In the following, definitions of comonads, Eilenberg–Moore and Kleisli objects in a 2-category are taken from
[
PW02
]
(see also
[
Str72
]
).

A
comonad
in the 2-category of indexed posets is a list
(P:\mathbb{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos},(K,\mathfrak{k}),\gamma,\varepsilon)
where
P
is a indexed poset,
(K,\mathfrak{k})
is a 1-arrow,
\gamma
and
\varepsilon
are 2-arrows, and
(K,\gamma,\varepsilon)
is a comonad in
\mathbb{C}
.
In particular, the following diagrams commute:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K(\gamma)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{K}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K(\varepsilon)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{K}}

.

Moreover, since
\gamma
and
\varepsilon
are 2-arrows, the following inequalities hold:

\mathfrak{k}_{A}(\alpha)\leq P(\gamma_{A})\mathfrak{k}_{KA}\mathfrak{k}_{A}(\alpha);\qquad\qquad\mathfrak{k}_{A}(\alpha)\leq P(\varepsilon_{A})(\alpha).

Before studying the Kleisli objects, we briefly recall Eilenberg–Moore objects for comonads.
Define the 2-category
\Cmd(\mathbf{IdxPos})
.

•

a
0-cell
is a comonad
(P:\mathbb{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos},(K,\mathfrak{k}),\gamma,\varepsilon)
;

•

a
1-cell
from the comonad
(P:\mathbb{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos},(K,\mathfrak{k}),\gamma,\varepsilon)
to
(P^{\prime}:{\mathbb{C}^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos},(K^{\prime},\mathfrak{k}^{\prime}),\gamma^{\prime},\varepsilon^{\prime})
is a lax morphism of comonads, i.e. a pair
((F,\mathfrak{f}),\mathfrak{j})
where the first entry
(F,\mathfrak{f}):P\to P^{\prime}
is a 1-arrow in
\mathbf{IdxPos}
and the second one
\mathfrak{j}:(F,\mathfrak{f})(K,\mathfrak{k})\to(K^{\prime},\mathfrak{k}^{\prime})(F,\mathfrak{f})
is a 2-arrow, i.e.
\mathfrak{j}:FK\xrightarrow{\cdot}K^{\prime}F
such that
\mathfrak{f}_{KA}\mathfrak{k}_{A}(\alpha)\leq P^{\prime}(\mathfrak{j}_{A})\mathfrak{k}^{\prime}_{FA}\mathfrak{f}_{A}(\alpha)
, satisfying the coherence diagrams below;

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FK}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{\prime}F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FK}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{\prime}F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FK^{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{\prime}FK}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{\prime 2}F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(\gamma)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma^{\prime}_{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(\varepsilon)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon^{\prime}_{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{j}_{K}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{\prime}(\mathfrak{j})}

•

a
2-cell
between
((F,\mathfrak{f}),\mathfrak{j})
and
((G,\mathfrak{g}),\mathfrak{h})
is a 2-arrow
\eta:(F,\mathfrak{f})\to(G,\mathfrak{g})
in
\mathbf{IdxPos}
, i.e.
\eta:F\xrightarrow{\cdot}G
such that
\mathfrak{f}_{A}(\alpha)\leq P^{\prime}(\eta_{A})\mathfrak{g}_{A}(\alpha)
, satisfying the coherence diagram below.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FK}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{\prime}F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces GK}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{\prime}G}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{K}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{\prime}(\eta)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{h}}

Definition 2.16
.

A 2-category
\chi
has
Eilenberg–Moore object for comonads
if the 2-functor
\inc:\chi\to\Cmd(\chi)
, which associates to every object the identity comonad, has a right 2-adjoint

(\mathrm{-})\text{-}\mathrm{Coalg}:\Cmd(\chi)\to\chi.

Proposition 2.17
.

The 2-category
\mathbf{IdxPos}
has Eilenberg–Moore object.

The proof of this result can be found in
[
DR21
]
. Here we just display how the right 2-adjoint
(\mathrm{-})\text{-}\mathrm{Coalg}:\Cmd(\mathbf{IdxPos})\to\mathbf{IdxPos}
is computed on 0-cells, since it will be useful for defining Kleisli objects: for a comonad
(P:\mathbb{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos},(K,\mathfrak{k}),\gamma,\varepsilon)
, define the functor
P^{K}:{\mathbb{C}^{K}}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
as follows. Let
\mathbb{C}^{K}
be the category of coalgebras for the comonad
(\mathbb{C},K,\gamma,\varepsilon)
in
\mathbf{Cat}
: its objects are pairs
(A,c)
where
A
is an object of
\mathbb{C}
and
c:A\to KA
is a
\mathbb{C}
-arrow such that
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces KA}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces KA}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces KA}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{2}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\par}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K(c)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\OPRid_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{A}}

,

while an arrow between
(A,c)
and
(B,d)
is a
\mathbb{C}
-arrow
f:A\to B
such that
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces KA}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces KB\par}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K(f)}

.

Let
P^{K}(A,c)
be
\{\alpha\in P(A)\mid\alpha\leq P(c)\mathfrak{k}_{A}(\alpha)\}
and
P^{K}(f)
be the restriction of
P(f)
.
We now define the 2-category
\Cmd^{*}(\mathbf{IdxPos}):=\Cmd(\mathbf{IdxPos}^{\mathrm{op}})^{\mathrm{op}}
.

•

a
0-cell
is a comonad
(P:\mathbb{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos},(K,\mathfrak{k}),\gamma,\varepsilon)
;

•

a
1-cell
from the comonad
(P:\mathbb{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos},(K,\mathfrak{k}),\gamma,\varepsilon)
to
(P^{\prime}:{\mathbb{C}^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos},(K^{\prime},\mathfrak{k}^{\prime}),\gamma^{\prime},\varepsilon^{\prime})
is an oplax morphism of comonads, i.e. a pair
((F,\mathfrak{f}),\mathfrak{j})
where the first entry
(F,\mathfrak{f}):P\to P^{\prime}
is a 1-arrow in
\mathbf{IdxPos}
and the second one
\mathfrak{j}:(K^{\prime},\mathfrak{k}^{\prime})(F,\mathfrak{f})\to(F,\mathfrak{f})(K,\mathfrak{k})
is a 2-arrow, i.e.
\mathfrak{j}:K^{\prime}F\xrightarrow{\cdot}FK
such that
\mathfrak{k}^{\prime}_{FA}\mathfrak{f}_{A}(\alpha)\leq P^{\prime}(\mathfrak{j}_{A})\mathfrak{f}_{KA}\mathfrak{k}_{A}(\alpha)
, satisfying the coherence diagrams below;

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{\prime}F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FK}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{\prime}F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FK}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{\prime 2}F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{\prime}FK}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FK^{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\par}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma^{\prime}_{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(\gamma)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon^{\prime}_{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(\varepsilon)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{\prime}(\mathfrak{j})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{j}_{K}}

•

a
2-cell
between
((F,\mathfrak{f}),\mathfrak{j})
and
((G,\mathfrak{g}),\mathfrak{h})
is a 2-arrow
\eta:(F,\mathfrak{f})\to(G,\mathfrak{g})
in
\mathbf{IdxPos}
, i.e.
\eta:F\xrightarrow{\cdot}G
such that
\mathfrak{f}_{A}(\alpha)\leq P^{\prime}(\eta_{A})\mathfrak{g}_{A}(\alpha)
, satisfying the coherence diagram below.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{\prime}F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FK}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{\prime}G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces GK\par}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{\prime}(\eta)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{K}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{h}}

Definition 2.18
.

A 2-category
\chi
has
Kleisli object for comonads
if the 2-functor associating to every object the identity comonad
\inc:\chi\to\Cmd^{*}(\chi)
, has a left 2-adjoint

(\mathrm{-})\text{-}\mathrm{coKl}:\Cmd^{*}(\chi)\to\chi.

We will devote the remaining of the section to prove Proposition
2.19
below. The reader may skip the proof and proceed directly to Section
3
, coming back here to look up the definition of the left 2-adjoint on 0-cells and of the universal arrow when needed.

Proposition 2.19
.

The 2-category
\mathbf{IdxPos}
has Kleisli object.

Proof.

In order to prove the statement, we shall explicitly construct the left 2-adjoint

(\mathrm{-})\text{-}\mathrm{coKl}:\Cmd^{*}(\mathbf{IdxPos})\to\mathbf{IdxPos}.

We obviously begin with

0-cells:

Fix a comonad
(P:\mathbb{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos},(K,\mathfrak{k}),\gamma,\varepsilon)
, and consider the functor
P_{K}:{\mathbb{C}_{K}}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
. Let
\mathbb{C}_{K}
be the category of free coalgebras for the comonad
(\mathbb{C},K,\gamma,\varepsilon)
in
\mathbf{Cat}
: it is the full subcategory of
\mathbb{C}^{K}
whose objects are pairs
(KA,\gamma_{A})
where
A
is an object of
\mathbb{C}
.
Let
P_{K}(KA,\gamma_{A})
be
P^{K}(KA,\gamma_{A})=\{\alpha\in P(KA)\mid\alpha\leq P(\gamma_{A})\mathfrak{k}_{KA}(\alpha)\}
and
P_{K}(f)
be
P^{K}(f)=P(f)
. This restriction is well defined because
P^{K}
is.

Remark 2.20
.

The category
\mathbb{C}_{K}
is isomorphic to the category
\overline{\mathbb{C}_{K}}
whose objects are the same as
\mathbb{C}
, and a
\overline{\mathbb{C}_{K}}
-arrow
A\rightsquigarrow B
is a
\mathbb{C}
-arrow
KA\to B
; composition between
g:A\rightsquigarrow B
and
h:B\rightsquigarrow C
is computed as

KA\xrightarrow{\gamma_{A}}K^{2}A\xrightarrow{K(g)}KB\xrightarrow{h}C;

the identity of
A
is given by
\varepsilon_{A}
.

The functor
{\mathbb{C}_{K}}\to\overline{\mathbb{C}_{K}}
sends
f:(KA,\gamma_{A})\to(KB,\gamma_{B})
to
\varepsilon_{B}f:A\rightsquigarrow B
.
The inverse
\overline{\mathbb{C}_{K}}\to\mathbb{C}_{K}
sends
g:A\rightsquigarrow B
to
K(g)\gamma_{A}
(for more details see, for example,
[
Bor94b
, Proposition 4.1.6]
for the proof of the dual statement concerning the Kleisli category of a monad).

We now resume the proof of Proposition
2.19
.

1-cells:

Consider
((F,\mathfrak{f}),\mathfrak{j}):(P,(K,\mathfrak{k}),\gamma,\varepsilon)\to(P^{\prime}:{\mathbb{C}^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos},(K^{\prime},\mathfrak{k}^{\prime}),\gamma^{\prime},\varepsilon^{\prime})
in
\Cmd^{*}(\mathbf{IdxPos})
. The corresponding 1-cell in
\mathbf{IdxPos}
will be
(F^{\prime},\mathfrak{f}^{\prime})
from
P_{K}
to
P^{\prime}_{K^{\prime}}
.
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{C}_{K}}^{\text{op}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{C}^{\prime}_{K^{\prime}}}^{\text{op}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{Pos}\par}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{op}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{K}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{P^{\prime}}_{K^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{f}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}

Define
F^{\prime}(KA,\gamma_{A}):=(K^{\prime}FA,\gamma^{\prime}_{FA})
, which is by definition a free
K^{\prime}
-coalgebra.

Then, take a morphism between free
K
-coalgebras
f:(KA,\gamma_{A})\to(KB,\gamma_{B})
, and let
F^{\prime}(f)
be
K^{\prime}(F(\varepsilon_{B}f)\mathfrak{j}_{A})\gamma^{\prime}_{FA}
.

K^{\prime}FA\xrightarrow{\mathfrak{j}_{A}}FKA\xrightarrow{F(f)}FKB\xrightarrow{F(\varepsilon_{B})}FB

F^{\prime}(f):=\bigg(K^{\prime}FA\xrightarrow{\gamma^{\prime}_{FA}}K^{\prime 2}FA\xrightarrow{K^{\prime}(F(\varepsilon_{B}f)\mathfrak{j}_{A})}K^{\prime}FB\bigg)

This is a morphism of
K^{\prime}
-coalgebras if and only if the following diagram commutes:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{\prime}FA}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{\prime}FB}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{\prime 2}FA}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{\prime 2}FB\par}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{\prime}(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma^{\prime}_{FA}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma^{\prime}_{FB}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{\prime}F^{\prime}(f)}

,

however

\gamma^{\prime}_{FB}K^{\prime}(F(\varepsilon_{B}f)\mathfrak{j}_{A})\gamma^{\prime}_{FA}=K^{\prime 2}(F(\varepsilon_{B}f)\mathfrak{j}_{A})\gamma^{\prime}_{K^{\prime}FA}\gamma^{\prime}_{FA}=K^{\prime 2}(F(\varepsilon_{B}f)\mathfrak{j}_{A})K^{\prime}(\gamma^{\prime}_{FA})\gamma^{\prime}_{FA},

using naturality of
\gamma^{\prime}
and its comultiplication property.

Consider that
\mathfrak{f}^{\prime}:P_{K}\xrightarrow{\cdot}{P^{\prime}}_{K^{\prime}}{F^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{op}}
, i.e. for any
(KA,\gamma_{A})
we look for

\mathfrak{f}^{\prime}_{(KA,\gamma_{A})}:{P_{K}(KA,\gamma_{A})}\to{P^{\prime}_{K^{\prime}}(K^{\prime}FA,\gamma^{\prime}_{FA})},

where
{P_{K}(KA,\gamma_{A})}{\subseteq PKA}
and
{P^{\prime}_{K^{\prime}}(K^{\prime}FA,\gamma^{\prime}_{FA})}{\subseteq P^{\prime}K^{\prime}FA}
.
So define
\mathfrak{f}^{\prime}_{(KA,\gamma_{A})}
to be the restriction of the following composition:

PKA\xrightarrow{\mathfrak{f}_{KA}}P^{\prime}FKA\xrightarrow{P^{\prime}(\mathfrak{j}_{A})}P^{\prime}K^{\prime}FA\xrightarrow{\mathfrak{k}^{\prime}_{K^{\prime}FA}}P^{\prime}K^{\prime 2}FA\xrightarrow{P^{\prime}(\gamma^{\prime}_{FA})}P^{\prime}K^{\prime}FA.

To prove that the restriction is well defined, take
\alpha\in P_{K}(KA,\gamma_{A})
, i.e.
\alpha\in PKA
such that
\alpha\leq P(\gamma_{A})\mathfrak{k}_{KA}(\alpha)
, we want to check that

\mathfrak{f}^{\prime}_{(KA,\gamma_{A})}(\alpha)\leq P^{\prime}(\gamma^{\prime}_{FA})\mathfrak{k}^{\prime}_{K^{\prime}FA}(\mathfrak{f}^{\prime}_{(KA,\gamma_{A})}(\alpha)).

This can be computed using that
\gamma^{\prime}:(K^{\prime},\mathfrak{k}^{\prime})\to(K^{\prime},\mathfrak{k}^{\prime})^{2}
is a 2-arrow, comultiplication property of
\gamma^{\prime}
and naturality of
\mathfrak{k}^{\prime}
.

Then we check naturality of
\mathfrak{f}^{\prime}
proving that for any
f:(KA,\gamma_{A})\to(KB,\gamma_{B})
, the following diagram commutes:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{K}(KB,\gamma_{B})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P^{\prime}_{K^{\prime}}(K^{\prime}FB,\gamma^{\prime}_{FB})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{K}(KA,\gamma_{A})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P^{\prime}_{K^{\prime}}(K^{\prime}FA,\gamma^{\prime}_{FA})\par}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{f}^{\prime}_{(KB,\gamma_{B})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P^{\prime}(K^{\prime}(F(\varepsilon_{B}f)\mathfrak{j}_{A})\gamma^{\prime}_{FA})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{f}^{\prime}_{(KA,\gamma_{A})}}

.

Observe that we can decompose the diagram above as follows:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces PKB}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P^{\prime}FKB}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P^{\prime}K^{\prime}FKB}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P^{\prime}K^{\prime 2}FB}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P^{\prime}K^{\prime}FB}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces PKA}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P^{\prime}FKA}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P^{\prime}K^{\prime}FKA}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P^{\prime}K^{\prime 2}FA}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P^{\prime}K^{\prime}FA\par}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{f}_{KB}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{k}^{\prime}_{FKB}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P^{\prime}F(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P^{\prime}K^{\prime}(\mathfrak{j}_{B})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P^{\prime}K^{\prime}F(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P^{\prime}(\gamma^{\prime}_{FB})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P^{\prime}(K^{\prime}(F(\varepsilon_{B}f)\mathfrak{j}_{A})\gamma^{\prime}_{FA})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{f}_{KA}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{k}^{\prime}_{FKA}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P^{\prime}K^{\prime}(\mathfrak{j}_{A})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P^{\prime}(\gamma^{\prime}_{FA})}

.

The first two squares commute because they are naturality squares of
\mathfrak{f}
and
\mathfrak{k}
respectively. To prove commutativity of the third square, it is enough to prove that the following square commutes:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{\prime}FA}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{\prime 2}FA}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{\prime}FKA}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{\prime}FB}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{\prime 2}FB}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{\prime}FKB}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma^{\prime}_{FA}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{\prime}(F(\varepsilon_{B}f)\mathfrak{j}_{A})\gamma^{\prime}_{FA}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{\prime}(\mathfrak{j}_{A})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{\prime}F(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma^{\prime}_{FB}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{\prime}(\mathfrak{j}_{B})}

and this can be shown by doing some computation using naturality of
\gamma^{\prime}
, naturality of
\mathfrak{j}
,
\gamma^{\prime}
comultiplication property,
\mathfrak{j}
coherence. In particular, we use that
K(\varepsilon_{B}f)\gamma_{A}=K(\varepsilon_{B})\gamma_{B}f=f
because of the definition of morphism between coalgebras and a property of
\varepsilon
.

2-cells:
Take a 2-cell
\eta:((F,\mathfrak{f}),\mathfrak{j})\to((G,\mathfrak{g}),\mathfrak{h})
, and look for
\eta^{\prime}:F^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\cdot}G^{\prime}
such that

\mathfrak{f}^{\prime}_{(KA,\gamma_{A})}(\alpha)\leq P^{\prime}_{K^{\prime}}(\eta^{\prime}_{(KA,\gamma_{A})})(\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}_{(KA,\gamma_{A})}(\alpha)).

Define
\eta^{\prime}_{(KA,\gamma_{A})}:(K^{\prime}FA,\gamma^{\prime}_{FA})\to(K^{\prime}GA,\gamma^{\prime}_{GA})
, as
\eta^{\prime}_{(KA,\gamma_{A})}:=K^{\prime}(\eta_{A})
.

Naturality diagram of
\gamma^{\prime}
applied to
\eta_{A}
proves that
\eta^{\prime}
is a morphism of coalgebras.

To prove naturality of
\eta^{\prime}
we have to check that for any
f:(KA,\gamma_{A})\to(KB,\gamma_{B})
the following diagram commutes:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(K^{\prime}FA,\gamma^{\prime}_{FA})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(K^{\prime}GA,\gamma^{\prime}_{GA})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(K^{\prime}FB,\gamma^{\prime}_{FB})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(K^{\prime}GB,\gamma^{\prime}_{GB})\par}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{\prime}(\eta_{A})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{\prime}(F(\varepsilon_{B}f)\mathfrak{j}_{A})\gamma^{\prime}_{FA}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{\prime}(G(\varepsilon_{B}f)\mathfrak{h}_{A})\gamma^{\prime}_{GA}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{\prime}(\eta_{B})}

but this follows from naturality of
\gamma^{\prime}
, coherence diagram of
\eta
and its naturality.

Finally,
\eta^{\prime}
is indeed a 2-arrow, i.e.
\mathfrak{f}^{\prime}_{(KA,\gamma_{A})}(\alpha)\leq P^{\prime}_{K^{\prime}}(\eta^{\prime}_{(KA,\gamma_{A})})(\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}_{(KA,\gamma_{A})}(\alpha))
, using that
\eta
is a 2-arrow, its coherence, naturality of
\mathfrak{k}
and naturality of
\gamma^{\prime}
.

Universal property:

In order to prove that
(\mathrm{-})\text{-coKl}:\Cmd^{*}(\mathbf{IdxPos})\to\mathbf{IdxPos}
is indeed a left adjoint, we have to find for each comonad
(P,(K,\mathfrak{k}),\gamma,\varepsilon)
a universal arrow

(P,(K,\mathfrak{k}),\gamma,\varepsilon)\longrightarrow(P_{K},(\OPRid,\OPRid),\OPRid,\OPRid)

i.e. a 1-arrow
((F_{K},\mathfrak{k}^{\prime}),\mathfrak{j}^{\prime})
such that, for any indexed poset
R:\mathbb{D}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
and any arrow

((F,\mathfrak{f}),\mathfrak{j}):(P,(K,\mathfrak{k}),\gamma,\varepsilon)\to\text{Inc}(R),

there exists a unique morphism
\overline{((F,\mathfrak{f}),\mathfrak{j})}
between the indexed posets
P_{K}
and
R
such that
((F,\mathfrak{f}),\mathfrak{j})=(\overline{((F,\mathfrak{f}),\mathfrak{j})},\OPRid)\circ((F_{K},\mathfrak{k}^{\prime}),\mathfrak{j}^{\prime})
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(P,(K,\mathfrak{k}),\gamma,\varepsilon)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(P_{K},(\OPRid,\OPRid),\OPRid,\OPRid)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(R,(\OPRid,\OPRid),\OPRid,\OPRid)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces((F,\mathfrak{f}),\mathfrak{j})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces((F_{K},\mathfrak{k}^{\prime}),\mathfrak{j}^{\prime})}

(2.1)

Define
(F_{K},\mathfrak{k}^{\prime}):P\to P_{K}
, where
F_{K}:\mathbb{C}\to\mathbb{C}_{K}
is the cofree functor

\bigg(f:A\to B\bigg)\longmapsto\bigg(K(f):(KA,\gamma_{A})\to(KB,\gamma_{B})\bigg)

and the natural transformation
\mathfrak{k}^{\prime}:P\xrightarrow{\cdot}P_{K}{F_{K}}^{\mathrm{op}}
is computed as
\mathfrak{k}
: this is well defined since, recalling that
\gamma
is a 2-arrow, we know that
\mathfrak{k}_{A}(\alpha)\leq P(\gamma_{A})\mathfrak{k}_{KA}\mathfrak{k}_{A}(\alpha)
, i.e.
\mathfrak{k}_{A}(\alpha)\in P_{K}F_{K}A
. Naturality of
\mathfrak{k}^{\prime}
follows from naturality of
\mathfrak{k}
.

Finally, define the 2-arrow
\mathfrak{j}^{\prime}:(F_{K},\mathfrak{k})\to(F_{K},\mathfrak{k})(K,\mathfrak{k})
to be
\mathfrak{j}^{\prime}_{A}:=\gamma_{A}
. This is natural and a 2-arrow because
\gamma
is.

Now consider
((F,\mathfrak{f}),\mathfrak{j})
; by definition of 1-cells in
\Cmd^{*}(\mathbf{IdxPos})
, we know that
(F,\mathfrak{f})
is a 1-arrow from
P
to
R
, and
\mathfrak{j}:F\xrightarrow{\cdot}FK
is such that
\mathfrak{f}_{A}(\alpha)\leq R(\mathfrak{j}_{A})\mathfrak{f}_{KA}\mathfrak{k}_{A}(\alpha)
, and the coherence diagrams become:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FK}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FK}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FK}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FK^{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\par}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(\gamma)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\OPRid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(\varepsilon)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{j}_{K}}

.

Define the functor
F^{\prime}:\mathbb{C}_{K}\to\mathbb{D}
to be the one that maps
f:(KA,\gamma_{A})\to(KB,\gamma_{B})
to the composition
F(\varepsilon_{B}f)\mathfrak{j}_{A}:FA\to FB
. We check that this is indeed a functor: for any pair of composable arrows
f:KA\to KB
,
g:KB\to KC
between free coalgebras we prove that
F^{\prime}(gf)=F^{\prime}(g)F^{\prime}(f)
using naturality of
\mathfrak{j}
, its coherence, definition of morphism between coalgebras and property of the counit. The identity is trivially preserved by
F^{\prime}
.

To conclude the definition of
\overline{((F,\mathfrak{f}),\mathfrak{j})}
, we have to find a natural transformation
\mathfrak{f}^{\prime}:P_{K}\xrightarrow{\cdot}R{F^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{op}}
. Define
\mathfrak{f}^{\prime}_{(KA,\gamma_{A})}:P_{K}(KA,\gamma_{A})\to RFA
to be the restriction of
R(\mathfrak{j}_{A})\mathfrak{f}_{KA}
. To show
\mathfrak{f}^{\prime}
is natural we need for any
f:KA\to KB
between free coalgebras that the following diagram commutes:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{K}(KB,\gamma_{B})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces RF^{\prime}(KB,\gamma_{B})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{K}(KA,\gamma_{A})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces RF^{\prime}(KA,\gamma_{A})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{f}^{\prime}_{(KB,\gamma_{B})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces RF^{\prime}(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{f}^{\prime}_{(KA,\gamma_{A})}}

.

To see this, observe that it is enough to prove the commutativity of the second square of
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces PKB}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces RFKB}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces RFB}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces PKA}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces RFKA}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces RFA}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{f}_{KB}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R(\mathfrak{j}_{B})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces RF(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R(F(\varepsilon_{B}f)\mathfrak{j}_{A})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{f}_{KA}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R(\mathfrak{j}_{A})}

,

since commutativity of the first square follows from naturality of
\mathfrak{f}
, but again it is enough to prove:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FA}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FKA}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FB}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FKB\par}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{j}_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(\varepsilon_{B}f)\mathfrak{j}_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{j}_{B}}

,

but this follows from naturality of
\mathfrak{j}
, its coherence, definition of morphism between coalgebras and counit property; so
\mathfrak{f}^{\prime}
is indeed a natural transformation.

We now prove that
((F^{\prime},\mathfrak{f}^{\prime}),\OPRid)\circ((F_{K},\mathfrak{k}),\gamma)=((F,\mathfrak{f}),\mathfrak{j})
.
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}^{\mathrm{op}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{C}_{K}}^{\mathrm{op}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{D}^{\mathrm{op}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{Pos}\par\par}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{K}}^{\mathrm{op}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{op}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{K}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{f}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}

The composition of the functors is indeed
F
:

\bigg(f:A\to B\bigg)\longmapsto\bigg(K(f):F_{K}A\to F_{K}B\bigg)\\
=\bigg(K(f):(KA,\gamma_{A})\to(KB,\gamma_{B})\bigg)\longmapsto\bigg(F(\varepsilon_{B}K(f))\mathfrak{j}_{A}:FA\to FB\bigg),

but
F(\varepsilon_{B}K(f))\mathfrak{j}_{A}=F(f\varepsilon_{A})\mathfrak{j}_{A}=F(f)
from naturality of
\varepsilon
and coherence of
\mathfrak{j}
.

Concerning the composition of the natural transformations, we need to check that
R(\mathfrak{j}_{A})\mathfrak{f}_{KA}\mathfrak{k}_{A}(\alpha)=\mathfrak{f}_{A}(\alpha)
. The direction
(\geq)
follows from the definition of
\mathfrak{j}
. To prove the converse, recall that coherence of
\mathfrak{j}
implies that
F(\varepsilon_{A})\mathfrak{j}_{A}
is the identity, so

\mathfrak{f}_{A}=R(\mathfrak{j}_{A})RF(\varepsilon_{A})\mathfrak{f}_{A}=R(\mathfrak{j}_{A})\mathfrak{f}_{KA}P(\varepsilon_{A})

from naturality of
\mathfrak{f}
. Moreover, since
\varepsilon
is a 2-arrow, we know that
\mathfrak{k}_{A}(\alpha)\leq P(\varepsilon_{A})(\alpha)
, so that
R(\mathfrak{j}_{A})\mathfrak{f}_{KA}\mathfrak{k}_{A}(\alpha)\leq R(\mathfrak{j}_{A})\mathfrak{f}_{KA}P(\varepsilon_{A})(\alpha)=\mathfrak{f}_{A}(\alpha)
, i.e.
(\leq)
holds.

The composition
(\OPRid\circ\gamma)_{A}
is
F^{\prime}(\gamma_{A})=F(\varepsilon_{KA}\gamma_{A})\mathfrak{j}_{A}=\mathfrak{j}_{A}
.

Finally, suppose that also
(G,\mathfrak{g})
is such that
((G,\mathfrak{g}),\OPRid)\circ((F_{K},\mathfrak{k}),\gamma)=((F,\mathfrak{f}),\mathfrak{j})
. Then in particular
GF_{K}=F
, so that
G=F^{\prime}
on objects; moreover,
\OPRid\circ\gamma=\mathfrak{j}
means that
G(\gamma_{A})=\mathfrak{j}_{A}
. Observe also that, given a
\mathbb{C}
-arrow
g
,
GK(g)=GF_{K}(g)=F(g)
. We claim that for any morphism
f:KA\to KB
between free coalgebras,
G(f)=F^{\prime}(f)
, i.e.
G(f)=F(\varepsilon_{B}f)\mathfrak{j}_{A}
.
First of all, coherence of
\mathfrak{j}
proves that

G(f)=F(\varepsilon_{B})\mathfrak{j}_{B}G(f)F(\varepsilon_{A})\mathfrak{j}_{A}.

However,
\mathfrak{j}_{B}G(f)F(\varepsilon_{A})=F(f)\mathfrak{j}_{A}F(\varepsilon_{A})
, so we obtain
G(f)=F(\varepsilon_{B})F(f)\mathfrak{j}_{A}F(\varepsilon_{A})\mathfrak{j}_{A}=F(\varepsilon_{B}f)\mathfrak{j}_{A}=F^{\prime}(f)
, i.e. the functor
G
is indeed the functor
F^{\prime}
.

To conclude, we have to prove that
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{f}^{\prime}
, i.e.
\mathfrak{g}_{(KA,\gamma_{A})}=R(\mathfrak{j}_{A})\mathfrak{f}_{KA}
. We know that
\mathfrak{g}\mathfrak{k}=\mathfrak{f}
, i.e.
\mathfrak{g}_{(KA,\gamma_{A})}\mathfrak{k}_{A}=\mathfrak{f}_{A}
, where
\mathfrak{g}_{(KA,\gamma_{A})}:P_{K}(KA,\gamma_{A})\to RFA
.

Note that

\displaystyle\mathfrak{f}^{\prime}_{(KA,\gamma_{A})}

\displaystyle=R(\mathfrak{j}_{A})\mathfrak{f}_{KA}=R(\mathfrak{j}_{A})\mathfrak{g}_{(K^{2}A,\gamma_{KA})}\mathfrak{k}_{KA}

\displaystyle=RG(\gamma_{A})\mathfrak{g}_{(K^{2}A,\gamma_{KA})}\mathfrak{k}_{KA}=\mathfrak{g}_{(KA,\gamma_{A})}P(\gamma_{A})\mathfrak{k}_{KA},

because of the property of the composition of
\mathfrak{k}
and
\mathfrak{g}
described above and naturality of
\mathfrak{g}
. We only need to prove that the composition
P(\gamma_{A})\mathfrak{k}_{KA}
acts like the identity on
P_{K}(KA,\gamma_{A})
. So take
\alpha\in PKA
such that
\alpha\leq P(\gamma_{A})\mathfrak{k}_{KA}(\alpha)
; we claim that
\alpha=P(\gamma_{A})\mathfrak{k}_{KA}(\alpha)
. Clearly
(\leq)
holds by definition, so we prove the converse. Recall that
\varepsilon
is a 2-arrow, so
\mathfrak{k}_{KA}(\alpha)\leq P(\varepsilon_{KA})(\alpha)
, and apply
P(\gamma_{A})
:

P(\gamma_{A})\mathfrak{k}_{KA}(\alpha)\leq P(\gamma_{A})P(\varepsilon_{KA})(\alpha)=\alpha

so we proved that
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{f}^{\prime}
and
((F_{K},\mathfrak{k}),\gamma)
is a universal arrow.

The isomorphism between the
Hom
-categories:

The adjunction we proved above induces a bijection on objects of the
Hom
-categories below for any indexed poset
R
and any comonad
(P,(K,\mathfrak{k}),\gamma,\varepsilon)\in\Cmd^{*}(\mathbf{IdxPos})
. We need to extend it on
2
-arrows and prove it is an isomorphism of categories.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{IdxPos}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Cmd^{*}(\mathbf{IdxPos})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Cmd^{*}(\mathbf{IdxPos})[(P,(K,\mathfrak{k}),\gamma,\varepsilon),\inc(R)]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{IdxPos}[P_{K},R]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\big((F,\mathfrak{f}),\mathfrak{j}\big)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(F^{\prime},R(\mathfrak{j})\mathfrak{f}_{K})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\big((G,\mathfrak{g}),\mathfrak{h}\big)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(G^{\prime},R(\mathfrak{h})\mathfrak{g}_{K})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathrm{-})\text{-coKl}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\inc}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

where
F^{\prime}\big(f:(KA,\gamma_{A})\to(KB,\gamma_{B})\big)=\big(F(\varepsilon_{B}f)\mathfrak{j}_{A}:FA\to FB\big)
, and similarly for
G^{\prime}
.

Take
\eta:\big((F,\mathfrak{f}),\mathfrak{j}\big)\to\big((G,\mathfrak{g}),\mathfrak{h}\big)
, i.e.

1.

\eta:F\xrightarrow{\cdot}G
is a natural transformation;

2.

\mathfrak{f}_{A}(\alpha)\leq P(\eta_{A})\mathfrak{g}_{A}(\alpha)
for any object
A
in
\mathbb{C}
and
\alpha\in PA
;

3.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces KF}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces KG}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{h}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{K}}

is commutative.

We prove that
\eta
in also a
2
-arrow between the correspondent indexed posets, defining
\eta_{(KA,\gamma_{A})}=\eta_{A}:FA\to GA
. It is a natural transformation between the functors
F^{\prime}
and
G^{\prime}
if for any
f:(KA,\gamma_{A})\to(KB,\gamma_{B})
, we have
\eta_{B}F(\varepsilon_{B}f)\mathfrak{j}_{A}=G(\varepsilon_{B}f)\mathfrak{h}_{A}\eta_{A}
. However:

\eta_{B}F(\varepsilon_{B}f)\mathfrak{j}_{A}=G(\varepsilon_{B}f)\eta_{KA}\mathfrak{j}_{A}=G(\varepsilon_{B}f)\mathfrak{h}_{A}\eta_{A}

using 1. and 3. Then we need
R(\mathfrak{j}_{A})\mathfrak{f}_{KA}(\alpha)\leq R(\eta_{(KA,\gamma_{A})})R(\mathfrak{h}_{A})\mathfrak{g}_{KA}(\alpha)
, but we know that

R(\eta_{A})R(\mathfrak{h}_{A})\mathfrak{g}_{KA}(\alpha)=R(\mathfrak{j}_{A})R(\eta_{KA})\mathfrak{g}_{KA}(\alpha),

so the inequality follows from 2.

To show this functor is full, take a natural transformation
\eta:F^{\prime}\to G^{\prime}
such that
R(\mathfrak{j}_{A})\mathfrak{f}_{KA}(\alpha)\leq R(\eta_{(KA,\gamma_{A})})R(\mathfrak{h}_{A})\mathfrak{g}_{KA}(\alpha)
for any
\alpha\in P(KA)
satisfying
\alpha\leq P(\gamma_{A})\mathfrak{k}_{KA}(\alpha)
, and we prove that 1., 2. and 3. hold. Take any
f:A\to B
, so that
K(f):(KA,\gamma_{A})\to(KB,\gamma_{B})
is a
\mathbb{C}_{K}
arrow; apply naturality to
K(f)
so
\eta_{(KB,\gamma_{B})}F^{\prime}(Kf)=G^{\prime}(Kf)\eta_{(KA,\gamma_{A})}
. However,
F^{\prime}(Kf)=F(\varepsilon_{B})FK(f)\mathfrak{j}_{A}=F(\varepsilon_{B})\mathfrak{j}_{B}F(f)=F(f)
, using naturality and coherence of
\mathfrak{j}
; similarly
G^{\prime}(Kf)=G(f)
, so
\eta
is a natural transformation from
F
to
G
. To show 2., take any
\beta\in PB
, we want
\mathfrak{f}_{B}(\beta)\leq R(\eta_{B})\mathfrak{g}_{B}(\beta)
, but by some computation we did above, we know that
\mathfrak{f}_{B}=R(\mathfrak{j}_{B})\mathfrak{f}_{KB}\mathfrak{k}_{B}
—and similarly
\mathfrak{g}_{B}=R(\mathfrak{h}_{B})\mathfrak{g}_{KB}\mathfrak{k}_{B}
—, so
\mathfrak{f}_{B}(\beta)=R(\mathfrak{j}_{B})\mathfrak{f}_{KB}\mathfrak{k}_{B}(\beta)\leq R(\eta_{B})R(\mathfrak{h}_{B})\mathfrak{g}_{KB}\mathfrak{k}_{B}(\beta)=R(\eta_{B})\mathfrak{g}_{B}(\beta)
, using the fact that
\mathfrak{k}_{B}(\beta)\in P_{K}(B)
and definition of
\eta
. Finally, observe that by definition of
\gamma
, we have that
\gamma_{A}:(KA,\gamma_{A})\to(K^{2}A,\gamma_{K^{2}A})
is a
\mathbb{C}_{K}
-arrow, so apply naturality of
\eta
with respect to
\gamma_{A}
to obtain
G^{\prime}(\gamma_{A})\eta_{(KA,\gamma_{A})}=\eta_{(K^{2}A,\gamma_{K^{2}A})}F^{\prime}(\gamma_{A})
, i.e.
G(\varepsilon_{KA}\gamma_{A})\mathfrak{h}_{A}\eta_{A}=\eta_{KA}F(\varepsilon_{KA}\gamma_{A})\mathfrak{j}_{A}
, hence
\mathfrak{h}_{A}\eta_{A}=\eta_{KA}\mathfrak{j}_{A}
, so that also 3. holds.
Again, faithfulness follows by definition, and it is essentially surjective because of the properties of adjunction. It is clear that the quasi-inverse is actually an inverse.∎

3
A comonad on the indexed poset
P

We now have all the ingredients to start our discussion concerning how to translate into the language of doctrines, seen as a generalization of the doctrine of well-formed formulae, the process of adding a constant of some fixed sort and adding a sentence to a theory.
We give an intuition of these two translations.

We start with the generalization of the concept of adding a constant. Constants in a first-order language are
0
-ary terms, terms are arrows in the category of contexts, the empty list of variables is the terminal object of the category of contexts, thus a
constant
for a doctrine is just an arrow with the terminal object in the base category of the doctrine as domain object; the codomain object of the constant is the
sort
of that constant. So, given an object
X
in the base category of a doctrine
P
,
adding a constant of sort
X
means, roughly speaking, defining from
P
a new doctrine
P_{X}
endowed with a constant of sort
X
for
P_{X}
, in a universal way. This construction and its universal property are formally stated in
Corollary

6.7
.

We then generalize the concept of adding an axiom. In first-order logic, we can add any sentence—that is, a formula with no free variables—to a theory, so that the sentence becomes a new axiom in the extended theory. Since the empty list of variables is the terminal object in the category of contexts, a
sentence
in a doctrine is just an element in the fiber over the terminal object in the base category, and an
axiom
in a doctrine is just a sentence that is the true constant in the fiber over the terminal object. So, given a sentence
\varphi
in a doctrine
P
,
adding the axiom
\varphi
means, roughly speaking, defining from
P
a new doctrine
P_{\varphi}
in which
\varphi
is the top element of the fiber over the terminal object—hence “
\varphi
is true”—, in a universal way. Observe that to do so, it is reasonable that the doctrine has at least the true constant, so we will work with primary doctrines—and not just with any doctrine, as we could do with the construction that adds a constant instead. This construction and its universal property are formally stated in
Corollary

6.5
.

As hinted in the introduction, although these two may seem like separate processes, we show that they can be computed simultaneously.

Notation 3.1
.

From now on,
P:\mathbb{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
is a fixed primary doctrine, unless otherwise specified.

Fix an object
X
in the base category
\mathbb{C}
, and an element
\varphi\in P(X)
.
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}^{\text{op}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}^{\text{op}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{Pos}\par}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(X\times\mathrm{-})^{\text{op}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}

Consider the product functor
X\times\mathrm{-}:\mathbb{C}\to\mathbb{C}
sending
(A\xrightarrow{f}B)
to
(X\times A\xrightarrow{\OPRid_{X}\times f}X\times B)
, and define each component of the natural transformation
\mathfrak{f}:P\xrightarrow{\cdot}P\circ(X\times\mathrm{-})^{\text{op}}
as follows:

\mathfrak{f}_{A}:P(A)\to P(X\times A)\text{, }\alpha\mapsto P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\varphi)\land P(\mathrm{pr}_{2})(\alpha)

where
\mathrm{pr}_{1}
and
\mathrm{pr}_{2}
are the projection from
X\times A
to
X
and
A
respectively.

Note that
\mathfrak{f}
is monotone and it is a natural transformation.
Then,
(X\times\mathrm{-},\mathfrak{f})
is a 1-cell between
P
and itself in the category
\mathbf{IdxPos}
.

We now prove that the 1-arrow
(X\times\mathrm{-},\mathfrak{f})
is part of a 2-comonad on
P
; to do this, we have to find two 2-arrows
\varepsilon:(X\times\mathrm{-},\mathfrak{f})\xrightarrow{\cdot}\OPRid_{P}
and
\gamma:(X\times\mathrm{-},\mathfrak{f})\xrightarrow{\cdot}(X\times\mathrm{-},\mathfrak{f})^{2}
satisfying the proper diagrams. We adapt the comonad on the functor
X\times\mathrm{-}
(also known as the
reader comonad
) to indexed posets.

Define
\varepsilon_{A}:X\times A\to A
to be the second projection
\varepsilon_{A}\coloneqq\mathrm{pr}_{2}
, which is clearly natural, and is indeed a 2-arrow since
\mathfrak{f}_{A}(\alpha)=P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\varphi)\land P(\mathrm{pr}_{2})(\alpha)\leq P(\mathrm{pr}_{2})(\alpha)=P(\varepsilon_{A})(\OPRid_{A}(\alpha))
for any
\alpha\in P(A)
. Then, define
\gamma_{A}\coloneqq\Delta_{X}\times\OPRid_{A}:X\times A\to X\times X\times A
, which is again natural; it is a 2-arrow if and only if
\mathfrak{f}_{A}(\alpha)\leq P(\Delta\times\OPRid)(\mathfrak{f}_{X\times A}\mathfrak{f}_{A}(\alpha))
, however

\displaystyle P(\Delta\times\OPRid)(\mathfrak{f}_{X\times A}\mathfrak{f}_{A}(\alpha))

\displaystyle=P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{2}\rangle)\big(P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})\varphi\land P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{2},\mathrm{pr}_{3}\rangle)(P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})\varphi\land P(\mathrm{pr}_{2})\alpha)\big)

\displaystyle=P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{2}\rangle)\big(P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})\varphi\land P(\mathrm{pr}_{2})\varphi\land P(\mathrm{pr}_{3})\alpha\big)

\displaystyle=P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})\varphi\land P(\mathrm{pr}_{2})\alpha=\mathfrak{f}_{A}(\alpha).

Finally, since the following diagrams commute:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times\mathrm{-}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times\mathrm{-}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(X\times\mathrm{-})^{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times\mathrm{-}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(X\times\mathrm{-})^{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times\mathrm{-}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(X\times\mathrm{-})^{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(X\times\mathrm{-})^{3}\par}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\OPRid_{X}\times\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{X\times\mathrm{-}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\OPRid_{X}\times\varepsilon}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{X\times\mathrm{-}}}

we proved the following

Proposition 3.2
.

With the notation defined above,
(P,(X\times\mathrm{-},\mathfrak{f}),\gamma,\varepsilon)
is a comonad in
\mathbf{IdxPos}
.

Remark 3.3
.

We are interested in finding a distributive law between two different comonads, both of the form seen in Proposition
3.2
on the same primary doctrine
P
seen as an indexed poset: for two objects
X,Y
and two elements
\varphi\in P(X),\psi\in P(Y)
, the first comonad relies on the 1-cell
(X\times\mathrm{-},\mathfrak{f})
, where
\mathfrak{f}=P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\varphi)\land P(\mathrm{pr}_{2})(\mathrm{-})
, while the second one relies on the 1-cell
(Y\times\mathrm{-},\mathfrak{g})
, where
\mathfrak{g}=P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\psi)\land P(\mathrm{pr}_{2})(\mathrm{-})
.

Dualizing distributive laws for monads in
[
Bec69
]
, recall that in general, for two given comonads
(P,(K,\mathfrak{f}),\gamma,\epsilon)
and
(P,(C,\mathfrak{g}),\lambda,\delta)
in
\mathbf{IdxPos}
on the same indexed poset
P:\mathbb{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
, a distributive law between two comonads is a 2-cell
\ell:(K,\mathfrak{f})\circ(C,\mathfrak{g})\to(C,\mathfrak{g})\circ(K,\mathfrak{f})
such that
((K,\mathfrak{f}),\ell)
is a lax morphism of comonads and
((C,\mathfrak{g}),\ell)
is an oplax morphism of comonads. In details, in
\mathbf{IdxPos}
, we ask for
\ell:KC\xrightarrow{\cdot}CK
to be a natural transformation, such that
\mathfrak{f}_{CA}\mathfrak{g}_{A}(\alpha)\leq P(\ell_{A})\mathfrak{g}_{KA}\mathfrak{f}_{A}(\alpha)
for any object
A
in
\mathbb{C}
and
\alpha\in P(A)
and such that the following diagram commute:

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

In our case, define for each object
A
in
\mathbb{C}
,

\ell_{A}\coloneqq\langle\mathrm{pr}_{2},\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{3}\rangle:X\times Y\times A\to Y\times X\times A.

This is trivially a natural transformation. Moreover, recall that the comultiplication is given by
\Delta\times\OPRid
, and the counit by the projection on the second component. With these definitions, the diagrams clearly commute:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X\times Y\times A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y\times X\times A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X\times Y\times Y\times A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y\times X\times Y\times A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y\times Y\times X\times A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\mathrm{pr}_{2},\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{3}\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{2},\mathrm{pr}_{2},\mathrm{pr}_{3}\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\mathrm{pr}_{2},\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{3},\mathrm{pr}_{4}\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{2},\mathrm{pr}_{3}\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{3},\mathrm{pr}_{2},\mathrm{pr}_{4}\rangle}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X\times Y\times A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y\times X\times A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X\times X\times Y\times A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X\times Y\times X\times A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y\times X\times X\times A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\mathrm{pr}_{2},\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{3}\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{2},\mathrm{pr}_{3}\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{3},\mathrm{pr}_{2},\mathrm{pr}_{4}\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{2},\mathrm{pr}_{2},\mathrm{pr}_{3}\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\mathrm{pr}_{2},\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{3},\mathrm{pr}_{4}\rangle}

Now we need to prove that
\ell
is indeed a 2-cell, i.e.

\hbox to256.18pt{\vbox to56.06pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 128.08833pt\lower-28.03192pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-17.38579pt}{-24.8736pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
