{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -22.03 -8.63 h 14.61 v 14.39 h -14.61 Z}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-13.77078pt}{-2.78458pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

Compute:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{f}_{CA}\mathfrak{g}_{A}(\alpha)

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{f}_{CA}\big(P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})\psi\land P(\mathrm{pr}_{2})\alpha\big)=P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})\varphi\land P(\mathrm{pr}_{2})\psi\land P(\mathrm{pr}_{3})\alpha

\displaystyle=P(\ell_{A})\big(P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})\psi\land P(\mathrm{pr}_{2})\varphi\land P(\mathrm{pr}_{3})\alpha\big)

\displaystyle=P(\ell_{A})\mathfrak{g}_{KA}\big(P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})\varphi\land P(\mathrm{pr}_{2})\alpha\big)=P(\ell_{A})\mathfrak{g}_{KA}\mathfrak{f}_{A}(\alpha).

In this particular case,
\ell
is actually an isomorphism.

By looking at the commutative triangles, we observe that
\ell
is unique:

\displaystyle\mathrm{pr}_{1}\ell_{A}=\mathrm{pr}_{1}\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{3}\rangle\ell_{A}=\mathrm{pr}_{1}\langle\mathrm{pr}_{2},\mathrm{pr}_{3}\rangle=\mathrm{pr}_{2};

\displaystyle\mathrm{pr}_{2}\ell_{A}=\mathrm{pr}_{1}\langle\mathrm{pr}_{2},\mathrm{pr}_{3}\rangle\ell_{A}=\mathrm{pr}_{1}\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{3}\rangle=\mathrm{pr}_{1};

\displaystyle\mathrm{pr}_{3}\ell_{A}=\mathrm{pr}_{2}\langle\mathrm{pr}_{2},\mathrm{pr}_{3}\rangle\ell_{A}=\mathrm{pr}_{2}\langle\mathrm{pr}_{2},\mathrm{pr}_{3}\rangle=\mathrm{pr}_{3}.

We conclude that the distributive law
\ell
induces a composite comonad, having the 1-cell computed as
(X\times\mathrm{-},\mathfrak{f})\circ(Y\times\mathrm{-},\mathfrak{g})=(X\times Y\times\mathrm{-},\mathfrak{f}\circ\mathfrak{g})
, where

(\mathfrak{f}\circ\mathfrak{g})_{A}=\mathfrak{f}_{Y\times A}\mathfrak{g}_{A}:P(A)\to P(X\times Y\times A),\quad\alpha\mapsto P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})\varphi\land P(\mathrm{pr}_{2})\psi\land P(\mathrm{pr}_{3})\alpha.

Moreover, the composite comonad induced by the distributive law is again of the form seen in Proposition
3.2
, defined with respect to the object
X\times Y
and the element
P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})\varphi\land P(\mathrm{pr}_{2})\psi
in
P(X\times Y)
.

4
The Kleisli construction for the comonad
(X\times\mathrm{-},\mathfrak{f})

We now study the Kleisli construction of the comonad
(P,(X\times\mathrm{-},\mathfrak{f}),\gamma,\varepsilon)
, applying the results shown in Section
2.2
to this particular case. To do so, we first have to compute the Eilenberg–Moore construction. The Eilenberg–Moore category
\mathbb{C}^{X\times\mathrm{-}}
has as objects pairs
(A,a)
, where
a:A\to X\times A
is an arrow in
\mathbb{C}
such that the following diagram commutes:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times X\times A\par}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{A}=\Delta\times\OPRid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{A}=\mathrm{pr}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\OPRid\times a}

so that the second component of
a=\langle a_{1},a_{2}\rangle
must be the identity
a_{2}=\OPRid_{A}
, while the first one can be any map
a_{1}:A\to X
. Moreover, an arrow
f:(A,a)\to(B,b)
in
\mathbb{C}^{X\times\mathrm{-}}
is an arrow
f:A\to B
in
\mathbb{C}
such that the diagram commutes:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times B\par}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle a_{1},\OPRid_{A}\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle b_{1},\OPRid_{B}\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\OPRid\times f}

i.e. we ask for the
\mathbb{C}
-arrow
f
to satisfy
b_{1}f=a_{1}
. From now on, we will write
\mathbb{C}^{X}
instead of
\mathbb{C}^{X\times\mathrm{-}}
. By looking at the description of objects and arrows of the category
\mathbb{C}^{X}
, it is easy to observe
\mathbb{C}^{X}
is isomorphic to the slice category
\mathbb{C}/X
: they are both categories of coalgebras of the reader comonad
X\times\mathrm{-}
.

As shown in Proposition
2.17
, the induced indexed poset
P^{(X,\varphi)}:{\mathbb{C}^{X}}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
is defined as follows:

For

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(B,b)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(A,a)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

the reindexing is

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{\beta\in P(B)\mid\beta\leq P(b)(\mathfrak{f}_{B}(\beta))\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{\alpha\in P(A)\mid\alpha\leq P(a)(\mathfrak{f}_{A}(\alpha))\}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P^{(X,\varphi)}(f)=P(f)_{\mid P^{(X,\varphi)}(B,b)}}

with the order of the subsets given by
P(B)
and
P(A)
respectively. Since by definition
\mathfrak{f}_{A}(\alpha)=P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\varphi)\land P(\mathrm{pr}_{2})(\alpha)
, we can write

\displaystyle P^{(X,\varphi)}(A,a)

\displaystyle=\{\alpha\in P(A)\mid\alpha\leq P(\langle a_{1},\OPRid\rangle)(P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\varphi)\land P(\mathrm{pr}_{2})(\alpha))\}

\displaystyle=\{\alpha\in P(A)\mid\alpha\leq P(a_{1})(\varphi)\}=P(A)_{\downarrow P(a_{1})(\varphi)}.

Example 4.1
.

To have an intuition of the Eilenberg-Moore construction, we compute the indexed poset
P^{(X,\varphi)}
when
P
is the the syntactic doctrine
\mathtt{LT}^{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathcal{T}}
in
Example

2.2
(c)
,
X
is a finite list of variables
\vec{x}
and
\varphi
is a formula
\varphi(\vec{x})
in the context
\vec{x}
. Objects of the base category are lists of terms
\vec{t}(\vec{z})
substitutable to
\vec{x}
, for some finite list of variables
\vec{z}
. An arrow in the base category between
\vec{t}(\vec{z})
and
\vec{s}(\vec{y})
is a list term
\vec{u}(\vec{z})
substitutable to
\vec{y}
such that
\vec{s}([\vec{u}(\vec{z})/\vec{y}])=\vec{t}(\vec{z})
. For a list
\vec{t}(\vec{z})
, the corresponding fiber is
\{\alpha(\vec{z})\in\mathtt{LT}^{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathcal{T}}(\vec{z})\mid\alpha(\vec{z})\vdash_{\mathcal{T}}\varphi([\vec{t}(\vec{z})/\vec{x}])\}
. Finally, the reindexing along
\vec{u}(\vec{z}):\vec{t}(\vec{z})\to\vec{s}(\vec{y})
maps
\beta(\vec{y})\in\mathtt{LT}^{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathcal{T}}(\vec{y})
such that
\beta(\vec{y})\vdash_{\mathcal{T}}\varphi([\vec{s}(\vec{y})/\vec{x}])
to
\beta([\vec{u}(\vec{z})/\vec{y}])\in\mathtt{LT}^{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathcal{T}}(\vec{z})
.

Now, consider the Kleisli category
\mathbb{C}_{X\times\mathrm{-}}
, i.e. the full subcategory of
\mathbb{C}^{X}
whose objects are the co-free algebras. From now on, we will write
\mathbb{C}_{X}
instead on
\mathbb{C}_{X\times\mathrm{-}}
. Observe that an arrow
f=\langle f_{1},f_{2}\rangle:X\times A\to X\times B
has to satisfy
\mathrm{pr}_{1}f=\mathrm{pr}_{1}
, so
f_{1}
must be the first projection
\mathrm{pr}_{1}
and the map
f
is uniquely determined by its second component
f_{2}:X\times A\to B
. For this reason, from now on we will use the equivalent description of
\mathbb{C}_{X}
, that has as objects the same as
\mathbb{C}
, and as map
g:A\rightsquigarrow B
is a
\mathbb{C}
-arrow
g:X\times A\to B
—see Remark
2.20
for more details; moreover, the composition between two arrows
g:A\rightsquigarrow B
and
h:B\rightsquigarrow C
is the arrow
h\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},g\rangle:A\rightsquigarrow C
. A new indexed poset
P_{(X,\varphi)}
is trivially induced on the Kleisli category by simply taking the restriction of
P^{(X,\varphi)}
on
{\mathbb{C}_{X}}^{\mathrm{op}}
, so that
P_{(X,\varphi)}:{\mathbb{C}_{X}}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
is defined as follows:

for

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

the reindexing is

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P(X\times B)_{\downarrow P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\varphi)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P(X\times A)_{\downarrow P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\varphi)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{(X,\varphi)}(g)=P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},g\rangle)_{\mid P_{(X,\varphi)}(B)}}
.

The Kleisli construction also comes with a 1-arrow between indexed posets
(F_{X},\mathfrak{f}):P\to P_{(X,\varphi)}
, where
F_{X}
is the co-free functor that sends
f:A\to B
to its precomposition with the second projection
f\mathrm{pr}_{2}:A\rightsquigarrow B
, and where each component of
\mathfrak{f}
is defined as
\mathfrak{f}_{A}=P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\varphi)\land P(\mathrm{pr}_{2})(\mathrm{-}):P(A)\to P_{(X,\varphi)}(A)
.

Remark 4.2
.

It is easy to see that for a fixed pair
X\in\mathbb{C},\varphi\in P(X)
, fiber
P(X\times A)_{\downarrow P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\varphi)}
of the doctrine
P_{(X,\varphi)}
over an object
A
is isomorphic to the quotient of
P(X\times A)
where
[\alpha]\leq[\beta]
if and only if
P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\varphi)\land\alpha\leq\beta
in
P(X\times A)
. One direction of the isomorphism takes any
\alpha\in P(X\times A)
,
\alpha\leq P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\varphi)
, and sends it to its equivalence class; the other direction takes any equivalence class
[\alpha]
for some
\alpha\in P(X\times A)
and sends it to the conjunction
P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\varphi)\land\alpha
of any representative with
P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\varphi)
.

Roughly speaking, if we think of
P
as the syntactic doctrine
\mathtt{LT}^{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathcal{T}}
in
Example

2.2
(c)
, we can interpret the intexed poset
P_{(X,\varphi)}
as follows. Suppose we want to add a new constant
c
to a first-order language
\mathcal{L}
and the formula
\varphi([c/x])
to the theory
\mathcal{T}
—thus we have a new axiom. We obtain a new language
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}=\mathcal{L}\cup\{c\}
and a new theory in the extended language
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}=\mathcal{T}\cup\{\varphi([c/x])\}
: the indexed poset
P_{(X,\varphi)}
should be thought as the syntactic doctrine
\mathtt{LT}^{\mathcal{L^{\prime}}}_{\mathcal{T^{\prime}}}
. Since adding a constant to a language clearly doesn’t change the lists of finite variables, the objects of the base category of
P_{(X,\varphi)}
are the same as the ones in the base category of
P
. A term
u(\vec{z})
in the language
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
is a term
u^{\prime}(\,(x);\vec{z}\,)
in the language
\mathcal{L}
with the constant
c
substituted to the variable
x
, thus a term
u(\vec{z}):\vec{z}\to(y)
in the extended language is just an old term
u^{\prime}(\,(x);\vec{z}\,):(\,(x);\vec{z}\,)\to(y)
evaluated in
[c/x]
. This is the intuition behind the fact that an arrow
A\rightsquigarrow B
in the base category
\mathbb{C}_{X}
of
P_{(X,\varphi)}
is just an old arrow
X\times A\to B
. In particular, we are able to pick the constant
c
added to the language as the
0
-ary term
c:()\to(x)
in the language
\mathcal{L^{\prime}}
, that is interpreted as
\mathrm{pr}_{1}:\mathbf{t}\rightsquigarrow X
.
Similarly, a formula
\alpha(\vec{z})
in the language
\mathcal{L^{\prime}}
is a formula
\alpha^{\prime}((x);\vec{z})
in the language
\mathcal{L}
with the constant
c
substituted to the variable
x
. This is why an element in
P_{(X,\varphi)}(A)
is in particular an element in
P(X\times A)
. To conclude, let
\alpha(\vec{z}),\beta(\vec{z})
be two formulae in the language
\mathcal{L^{\prime}}
and let
\alpha^{\prime}((x);\vec{z}),\beta^{\prime}((x);\vec{z})
be formulae in the language
\mathcal{L}
such that
\alpha(\vec{z})=\alpha^{\prime}([c/x];\vec{z})
and
\beta(\vec{z})=\beta^{\prime}([c/x];\vec{z})
. Then

\displaystyle\alpha(\vec{z})\vdash_{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}\beta(\vec{z})

\displaystyle\text{\quad if and only if\quad}\varphi([c/x])\land\alpha(\vec{z})\vdash_{\mathcal{T}}\beta(\vec{z})

\displaystyle\text{\quad if and only if\quad}\varphi([c/x])\land\alpha^{\prime}([c/x];\vec{z})\vdash_{\mathcal{T}}\beta^{\prime}([c/x];\vec{z})

\displaystyle\text{\quad if and only if\quad}\varphi(x)\land\alpha^{\prime}((x);\vec{z})\vdash_{\mathcal{T}}\beta^{\prime}((x);\vec{z}).

This is the intuition behind the presentation of the fibers of
P_{(X,\varphi)}
described in
Remark

4.2
above.
These ideas will be formally stated and proved in
Examples

6.11
and
6.12
, but for now they are convenient intuitions to keep in mind while reading the rest of the paper.

5
The doctrine
P_{(X,\varphi)}
and its inherited properties

We now want to study if some properties of
P
can be translated to
P_{(X,\varphi)}
, and when so, if they are preserved by the 1-arrow
(F_{X},\mathfrak{f})
.
First of all, we check that
\mathbb{C}_{X}
has finite products, preserved by
F_{X}
, so that both
P
and
P_{(X,\varphi)}
are doctrines and
(F_{X},\mathfrak{f})
is a 1-arrow in
\mathbf{Dct}
. Then we study a few other properties of
\mathbb{C}_{X}
that can be inherited from
\mathbb{C}
; then we will take a look to various properties of
P
.
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}^{\text{op}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{C}_{X}}^{\text{op}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{Pos}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{X}}^{\text{op}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{(X,\varphi)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}

We begin by collecting some elementary results regarding the category
\mathbb{C}_{X}
. The key part is that
\mathbb{C}_{X}
has products, so that
P_{(X,\varphi)}
is a doctrine. The part of the statement about products follows from the dual of Proposition 2.2 in
[
Szi83
]
. We then provide the proof of the other properties, as we could not find precise references.

Proposition 5.1
.

Let
\mathbb{C}
be a category with finite products, and
\mathbb{C}_{X}
be the Kleisli category of the comonad
(\mathbb{C},X\times\mathrm{-},\Delta_{X}\times\OPRid,\mathrm{pr}_{2})
. Then the category
\mathbb{C}_{X}
has finite products, and the co-free functor
F_{X}:\mathbb{C}\to\mathbb{C}_{X}
preserves finite products.
Moreover:

1.

If the category
\mathbb{C}
is closed, then
\mathbb{C}_{X}
is closed and the co-free functor
F_{X}:\mathbb{C}\to\mathbb{C}_{X}
preserves the exponential.

2.

Suppose that
\mathbb{C}
has initial object
I
. The category
\mathbb{C}_{X}
has initial object and the co-free functor
F_{X}:\mathbb{C}\to\mathbb{C}_{X}
preserves the initial object if and only if the endofunctor
X\times\mathrm{-}:\mathbb{C}\to\mathbb{C}
preserves the initial object.

3.

Suppose that
\mathbb{C}
has binary coproducts. The category
\mathbb{C}_{X}
has binary coproducts and the co-free functor
F_{X}:\mathbb{C}\to\mathbb{C}_{X}
preserves binary coproducts if and only if the endofunctor
X\times\mathrm{-}:\mathbb{C}\to\mathbb{C}
preserves binary coproducts.

Proof.

1.

Suppose that for any object
Y
there is a natural bijection

\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathrm{-}\times Y,\mathrm{=})\cong\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathrm{-},(\mathrm{=})^{Y}).

We sum up the naturality in the two components with the following diagrams:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\times Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\times Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\times Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z^{Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z^{Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S^{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\times\OPRid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{h}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{Y}}

Consider the functor
\mathrm{-}\times Y:\mathbb{C}_{X}\to\mathbb{C}_{X}
, that maps
f:A\rightsquigarrow B
, i.e.
f:X\times A\to B
, to the arrow
f\times\OPRid:A\times Y\rightsquigarrow B\times Y
, i.e.
f\times\OPRid:X\times A\times Y\to B\times Y
. Such functor is a left adjoint, since for each object
A
, there exists an object
A^{Y}
—which we will prove to be the exponential in
\mathbb{C}
—and a
\mathbb{C}_{X}
-arrow
\varepsilon_{A}:A^{Y}\times Y\rightsquigarrow A
, i.e.
\varepsilon_{A}:X\times A^{Y}\times Y\to A
such that, for any object
B
and arrow
f:B\times Y\rightsquigarrow A
, there exists a unique
\widehat{f}:B\rightsquigarrow A^{Y}
—which we will prove to be the same hatted arrow in
\mathbb{C}
—such that
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\times Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{Y}\times Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{f}\times\OPRid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{A}}

Define
\varepsilon_{A}\coloneqq\widetilde{\mathrm{pr}_{2}}:X\times A^{Y}\times Y\to A
, the
\mathbb{C}
-map corresponding to
\mathrm{pr}_{2}:X\times A^{Y}\to A^{Y}
, so that
\varepsilon_{A}
is indeed a
\mathbb{C}_{X}
-map
A^{Y}\times Y\rightsquigarrow A
. We only have to check that the composition of
\mathbb{C}_{X}
-arrows above equals to
f
, i.e. in
\mathbb{C}

\widetilde{\mathrm{pr}_{2}}\circ\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\widehat{f}\times\OPRid\rangle=\widetilde{\mathrm{pr}_{2}}\circ(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\widehat{f}\rangle\times\OPRid)=\widetilde{\mathrm{pr}_{2}\circ\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\widehat{f}\rangle}=\widetilde{\widehat{f}}=f

At last, to prove the uniqueness of
\widehat{f}
, suppose
f^{\prime}
such that
\widetilde{\mathrm{pr}_{2}}\circ\langle q_{1},f^{\prime}\times\OPRid\rangle=f
, but the left-hand side is equal to
\widetilde{f^{\prime}}
, so
\widehat{\widetilde{f^{\prime}}}=\widehat{f}
, i.e.
f^{\prime}=\widehat{f}
.

To conclude, take a
\mathbb{C}
-arrow
g:A\times Y\to B
, and its corresponding map
\widehat{g}:A\to B^{Y}
, we want to prove that
\widehat{F_{X}(g)}=F_{X}(\widehat{g})
.

F_{X}(g):X\times A\times Y\xrightarrow{\langle\mathrm{pr}_{2},\mathrm{pr}_{3}\rangle}A\times Y\xrightarrow{g}B

F_{X}(\widehat{g}):X\times A\xrightarrow{\mathrm{pr}_{2}}A\xrightarrow{\widehat{g}}B^{Y}

By naturality we have:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times A\times Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\times Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{pr}_{2}\times\OPRid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{pr}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{g}}

i.e.
\widetilde{\widehat{g}\mathrm{pr}_{2}}=g(\mathrm{pr}_{2}\times\OPRid)=g\langle\mathrm{pr}_{2},\mathrm{pr}_{3}\rangle
, so that
\widehat{g}\mathrm{pr}_{2}=\widehat{g\langle\mathrm{pr}_{2},\mathrm{pr}_{3}\rangle}
.

2.

We show that
F_{X}I=I
is initial in
\mathbb{C}_{X}
: consider any object
A
, we look for a unique arrow
I\rightsquigarrow A
, i.e. a unique arrow
X\times I\to A
, but
X\times I=I
by assumption.
Conversely, suppose that
\mathbb{C}_{X}
has initial object, preserved by
F_{X}
. Since
U_{X}:\mathbb{C}_{X}\to\mathbb{C}
, that acts
\big(g:C\rightsquigarrow D\big)\mapsto\big(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},g\rangle:X\times C\to X\times D\big)
, is a left adjoint, it preserves all colimits, and in particular
X\times I=I
, as claimed.

3.

At first we suppose that the functor
X\times\mathrm{-}
preserves binary coproducts. Consider any pair of objects
A
,
B
, their coproduct diagram in
\mathbb{C}
, its image in
\mathbb{C}_{X}
through
F_{X}
and a pair of arrows
\alpha:X\times A\to V
and
\beta:X\times B\to V
:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A+B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A+B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{A}\mathrm{pr}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{B}\mathrm{pr}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}

Define the map
A+B\rightsquigarrow V
to be the composition
\binom{\alpha}{\beta}\psi:X\times(A+B)\xrightarrow[\sim]{\psi}(X\times A)+(X\times B)\xrightarrow{\binom{\alpha}{\beta}}V
, where
\psi
is the inverse of the canonical arrow below:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(X\times A)+(X\times B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times(A+B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(A+B)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{X\times A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{X\times B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\langle\binom{\mathrm{pr}_{1}}{\mathrm{pr}_{1}},\binom{\iota_{A}\mathrm{pr}_{2}}{\iota_{B}\mathrm{pr}_{2}}\rangle=\binom{\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\iota_{A}\mathrm{pr}_{2}\rangle}{\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\iota_{B}\mathrm{pr}_{2}\rangle}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{pr}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{pr}_{2}}

In particular,

\binom{\iota_{X\times A}}{\iota_{X\times B}}=\OPRid_{(X\times A)+(X\times B)}=\psi\binom{\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\iota_{A}\mathrm{pr}_{2}\rangle}{\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\iota_{B}\mathrm{pr}_{2}\rangle}=\binom{\psi\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\iota_{A}\mathrm{pr}_{2}\rangle}{\psi\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\iota_{B}\mathrm{pr}_{2}\rangle}.

So the composition
X\times A\xrightarrow{\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\iota_{A}\mathrm{pr}_{2}\rangle}X\times(A+B)\xrightarrow{\binom{\alpha}{\beta}\psi}V
is equal to
\binom{\alpha}{\beta}\psi\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\iota_{A}\mathrm{pr}_{2}\rangle=\binom{\alpha}{\beta}\iota_{X\times A}=\alpha
; similarly, for
\beta
. Hence,
\binom{\alpha}{\beta}\psi
makes the diagram commute, and it is clearly unique, so
\mathbb{C}_{X}
has coproducts, preserved by
F_{X}
by construction.

Conversely, suppose that
\mathbb{C}_{X}
has coproducts, preserved by
F_{X}
, our claim is that in
\mathbb{C}
,
X\times\mathrm{-}
distribute over
+
. So in
\mathbb{C}_{X}
take
A,B
and their coproduct
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(A+B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{A}\mathrm{pr}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{B}\mathrm{pr}_{2}}

Recall that
U_{X}:\mathbb{C}_{X}\to\mathbb{C}
, that maps
g:C\rightsquigarrow D
to
\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},g\rangle:X\times C\to X\times D
is a left adjoint, so it preserves all colimits, and in particular
X\times(A+B)=(X\times A)+(X\times B)
, as claimed.∎

Remark 5.2
.

Is it important to observe that the hypothesis about
F_{X}
preserving coproducts is necessary for the equivalence in 3. of the proposition above. Indeed, suppose
\mathbb{C}
to be a bounded lattice
(R,\land,\lor)
, and fix
x\in R
; so
\mathbb{C}_{X}=\overline{R}\coloneqq(|R|,\sqsubset)
where
a\sqsubset a^{\prime}
if and only if
x\land a\leq a^{\prime}
. The poset
\overline{R}
has coproducts: indeed,
a\overline{\lor}b\coloneqq(x\land a)\lor(x\land b)
. Clearly
a\sqsubset a\overline{\lor}b
and
b\sqsubset a\overline{\lor}b
; moreover, take
a,b\sqsubset y
, i.e.
x\land a\leq y
and
x\land b\leq y
, then
a\overline{\lor}b\sqsubset y
if and only if

x\land({{(x\land a)}\lor{(x\land b)}})\leq y,

which holds, so that
\overline{R}
has indeed coproducts. However, if
x\land\mathrm{-}
does not distribute over
\lor
, coproducts are not preserved—
x\land(a\lor b)\neq(x\land a)\lor(x\land b)
.

We now study the structural properties of the fibers of
P
that are inherited by
P_{(X,\varphi)}
and preserved by the morphism
(F_{X},\mathfrak{f})
.

Finite meets

Proposition 5.3
.

Let
P:\mathbb{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
be a primary doctrine and
P_{(X,\varphi)}
be the Kleisli object of the comonad
(P,(X\times\mathrm{-},\mathfrak{f}),\gamma,\varepsilon)
defined by the pair
X\in\mathbb{C}
and
\varphi\in P(X)
. Then
P_{(X,\varphi)}
is a primary doctrine, and
(F_{X},\mathfrak{f})
is a primary homomorphism.

Proof.

Recall that, by assumption, for any object
A
of
\mathbb{C}
, the poset
P(A)
has finite meets. We want to check that
P_{(X,\varphi)}(A)=P(X\times A)_{\downarrow P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\varphi)}
has finite meets too: for any two elements
\alpha,\beta\in P_{(X,\varphi)}(A)
, their meet is
\alpha\land\beta
computed in
P(X\times A)
. Naturality of the operation is trivial. Moreover, the poset
P_{(X,\varphi)}(A)
has a top element, which is
\mathsf{1}_{A}\coloneqq P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\varphi)
, and
\mathsf{1}
is again natural.

Take
\mathfrak{f}_{A}:P(A)\to P_{(X,\varphi)}(A)=P(X\times A)_{\downarrow P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\varphi)}
. For any
\alpha,\beta\in P(A)
, one has
\mathfrak{f}_{A}(\alpha\land\beta)=P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\varphi)\land P(\mathrm{pr}_{2})(\alpha\land\beta)=P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\varphi)\land P(\mathrm{pr}_{2})(\alpha)\land P(\mathrm{pr}_{2})(\beta)=\mathfrak{f}_{A}(\alpha)\land\mathfrak{f}_{A}(\beta)
. Moreover,
\mathfrak{f}_{A}(\top_{A})=P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\varphi)\land P(\mathrm{pr}_{2})(\top_{A})=P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\varphi)\land\top_{X\times A}=P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\varphi)=\mathsf{1}_{A}
.∎

Elementarity

Proposition 5.4
.

Let
P:\mathbb{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
be a primary doctrine and
P_{(X,\varphi)}
be the Kleisli object of the comonad
(P,(X\times\mathrm{-},\mathfrak{f}),\gamma,\varepsilon)
defined by the pair
X\in\mathbb{C}
and
\varphi\in P(X)
. If
P
is an elementary doctrine, then
P_{(X,\varphi)}
is an elementary doctrine, and
(F_{X},\mathfrak{f})
is an elementary homomorphism.

Proof.

We already proved that, since
P
is a primary doctrine,
P_{(X,\varphi)}
is a primary doctrine too. So now take an object
A
and define

\delta^{X,\varphi}_{A}:=P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\varphi)\land P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{2},\mathrm{pr}_{3}\rangle)(\delta_{A})\in P_{(X,\varphi)}(A\times A)=P(X\times A\times A)_{\downarrow P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\varphi)},

where
\delta_{A}\in P(A\times A)
is the fibered equality of
P
on
A
.

1.

We show that in
P_{(X,\varphi)}(A)=P(X\times A)_{\downarrow P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\varphi)}
we have
P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\varphi)\leq P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{2},\mathrm{pr}_{2}\rangle)(\delta^{X,\varphi}_{A})
, so we compute:

\displaystyle P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{2},\mathrm{pr}_{2}\rangle)(\delta^{X,\varphi}_{A})

\displaystyle=P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\varphi)\land P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{2},\mathrm{pr}_{2}\rangle)(\delta_{A})=P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\varphi)\land P(\mathrm{pr}_{2})P(\Delta_{A})(\delta_{A})

\displaystyle=P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\varphi)\land P(\mathrm{pr}_{2})(\top_{A})=P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\varphi)

using Definition
2.6
.1.

2.

For any
\alpha\in P_{(X,\varphi)}(A)
, we show that in
P_{(X,\varphi)}(A\times A)=P(X\times A\times A)_{\downarrow P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\varphi)}
we have
P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{2}\rangle)(\alpha)\land P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\varphi)\land P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{2},\mathrm{pr}_{3}\rangle)(\delta_{A})\leq P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{3}\rangle)(\alpha)
.
To see this, observe that by property 2. with respect to the object
X\times A
in Definition
2.6
, in the fiber
P(X\times A\times X\times A)
we have

P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{2}\rangle)(\alpha)\land\delta_{X\times A}\leq P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{3},\mathrm{pr}_{4}\rangle)(\alpha).

Then apply
P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{2},\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{3}\rangle)
to both sides of the inequality to get that in
P(X\times A\times A)

P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{2}\rangle)(\alpha)\land P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{2},\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{3}\rangle)(\delta_{X\times A})\leq P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{3}\rangle)(\alpha).

However, using properties 3. and 1. in Definition
2.6
we compute

\displaystyle P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{2},\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{3}\rangle)(\delta_{X\times A})

\displaystyle\geq P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{2},\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{3}\rangle)\big(P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{3}\rangle)(\delta_{X})\land P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{2},\mathrm{pr}_{4}\rangle)(\delta_{A})\big)

\displaystyle=P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{1}\rangle)(\delta_{X})\land P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{2},\mathrm{pr}_{3}\rangle)(\delta_{A})

\displaystyle=P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})P(\Delta_{X})(\delta_{X})\land P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{2},\mathrm{pr}_{3}\rangle)(\delta_{A})

\displaystyle=P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{2},\mathrm{pr}_{3}\rangle)(\delta_{A}).

In conclusion we get:

\displaystyle P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{2}\rangle)(\alpha)\land P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\varphi)\land P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{2},\mathrm{pr}_{3}\rangle)(\delta_{A})

\displaystyle\leq P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{2}\rangle)(\alpha)\land P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{2},\mathrm{pr}_{3}\rangle)(\delta_{A})

\displaystyle\leq P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{2}\rangle)(\alpha)\land P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{2},\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{3}\rangle)(\delta_{X\times A})

\displaystyle\leq P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{3}\rangle)(\alpha)

as claimed.

3.

For any pair of objects
A,B
, we show that in
P_{(X,\varphi)}(A\times B\times A\times B)=P(X\times A\times B\times A\times B)_{\downarrow P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\varphi)}
we have
P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{2},\mathrm{pr}_{4}\rangle)(\delta^{X,\varphi}_{A})\land P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{3},\mathrm{pr}_{5}\rangle)(\delta^{X,\varphi}_{B})\leq\delta^{X,\varphi}_{A\times B}
, i.e.

P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\varphi)\land P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{2},\mathrm{pr}_{4}\rangle)(\delta_{A})\land P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\varphi)\land P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{3},\mathrm{pr}_{5}\rangle)(\delta_{B})\\
\leq P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\varphi)\land P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{2},\mathrm{pr}_{3},\mathrm{pr}_{4},\mathrm{pr}_{5}\rangle)(\delta_{A\times B}),

but this holds by applying
P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{2},\mathrm{pr}_{3},\mathrm{pr}_{4},\mathrm{pr}_{5}\rangle)
to both sides of the inequality 3. in Definition
2.6
.

This proves that
P_{(X,\varphi)}
is an elementary doctrine. To conclude, we show that
(F_{X},\mathfrak{f})
is an elementary morphism, but this follows from Proposition
5.3
and from the definition of the fibered equality on
A
as
\delta^{X,\varphi}_{A}=\mathfrak{f}_{A\times A}(\delta_{A})
.
∎

Existential quantifier

Proposition 5.5
.

Let
P:\mathbb{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
be a primary doctrine and
P_{(X,\varphi)}
be the Kleisli object of the comonad
(P,(X\times\mathrm{-},\mathfrak{f}),\gamma,\varepsilon)
defined by the pair
X\in\mathbb{C}
and
\varphi\in P(X)
. If
P
is an existential doctrine, then
P_{(X,\varphi)}
is an existential doctrine, and
(F_{X},\mathfrak{f})
is an existential homomorphism.

Proof.

We already proved that, since
P
is primary,
P_{(X,\varphi)}
is primary as well. So now take objects
B,C
and consider
P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{2}\rangle):P(X\times C)_{\downarrow P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\varphi)}\to P(X\times C\times B)_{\downarrow P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\varphi)}
, and define its left adjoint
{\exists_{X,\varphi}}^{B}_{C}
to be the restriction of
\exists^{B}_{X\times C}
. Such restriction is well defined: take
\beta\leq P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\varphi)
in
P(X\times C\times B)
, one has
\exists^{B}_{X\times C}(\beta)\leq P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\varphi)
if and only if
\beta\leq P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{2}\rangle)P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\varphi)=P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\varphi)
, which is true by assumption. Beck-Chevalley condition and Frobenius reciprocity for
{\exists_{X,\varphi}}^{B}_{C}
come easy from the same properties of
\exists^{B}_{X\times C}
.

Consider the following diagram:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P(C\times B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P(X\times C\times B)_{\downarrow P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\varphi)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P(C)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P(X\times C)_{\downarrow P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\varphi)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{f}_{C\times B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists^{B}_{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\exists_{X,\varphi}}^{B}_{C}=\exists^{B}_{X\times C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{f}_{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{2}\rangle)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vdash}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vdash}

.

We want to prove that the square with arrows pointing down and right is commutative. To do this, recall that the following diagram is commutative because of the Beck-Chevalley condition:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P(C\times B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P(C)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P(X\times C\times B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P(X\times C)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P(\mathrm{pr}_{2}\times\OPRid_{B})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists^{B}_{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P(\mathrm{pr}_{2})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists^{B}_{X\times C}}

.

So now take
\beta\in P(C\times B)
:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{f}_{C}\exists^{B}_{C}(\beta)

\displaystyle=P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\varphi)\land P(\mathrm{pr}_{2})(\exists^{B}_{C}(\beta))=P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\varphi)\land\exists^{B}_{X\times C}{P(\mathrm{pr}_{2}\times\OPRid)}(\beta)

\displaystyle=\exists^{B}_{X\times C}(P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{2},\mathrm{pr}_{3}\rangle)(\beta)\land{P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{2}\rangle)P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})}(\varphi))=\exists^{B}_{X\times C}\mathfrak{f}_{X\times C}(\beta),

which proves our claim.∎

Universal quantifier

Proposition 5.6
.

Let
P:\mathbb{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
be a primary doctrine and
P_{(X,\varphi)}
be the Kleisli object of the comonad
(P,(X\times\mathrm{-},\mathfrak{f}),\gamma,\varepsilon)
defined by the pair
X\in\mathbb{C}
and
\varphi\in P(X)
. If
P
is a universal doctrine, then
P_{(X,\varphi)}
is a universal doctrine, and
(F_{X},\mathfrak{f})
is a universal homomorphism.

Additionally, if the universal quantifier of
P
satisfies the Frobenius reciprocity, then also the universal quantifier of
P_{(X,\varphi)}
does.

Proof.

Take a pair of objects
B,C
and consider

P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{2}\rangle):P(X\times C)_{\downarrow P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\varphi)}\to P(X\times C\times B)_{\downarrow P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\varphi)}.

Define its right adjoint
{\forall_{X,\varphi}}^{B}_{C}(\mathrm{-})\coloneqq\forall^{B}_{X\times C}(\mathrm{-})\land P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\varphi)
. To check that this yields indeed an adjunction, take
\gamma\leq P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\varphi)
in
P(X\times C)
and
\beta\leq P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\varphi)
in
P(X\times C\times B)
, we want to prove that
P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{2}\rangle)(\gamma)\leq\beta
if and only if
\gamma\leq{\forall_{X,\varphi}}^{B}_{C}(\beta)=\forall^{B}_{X\times C}(\beta)\land P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\varphi)
. First of all, suppose
P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{2}\rangle)(\gamma)\leq\beta
, then it follows from the adjunction
P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{2}\rangle)\dashv\forall^{B}_{X\times C}(\beta)
that
\gamma\leq\forall^{B}_{X\times C}(\beta)
; combining this with the assumption on
\gamma
, the inequality
\gamma\leq{\forall_{X,\varphi}}^{B}_{C}(\beta)
holds. Conversely, suppose
\gamma\leq\forall^{B}_{X\times C}(\beta)\land P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\varphi)\leq\forall^{B}_{X\times C}(\beta)
, then the claim holds again because of the adjunction.

We now want to prove the Beck-Chevalley condition for
{\forall_{X,\varphi}}
. Take an arrow
f_{2}:C^{\prime}\rightsquigarrow C
, i.e.
f_{2}:X\times C^{\prime}\to C
, and write
f\coloneqq\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},f_{2}\rangle:X\times C^{\prime}\to X\times C
. Recall the Beck-Chevalley condition for the universal quantifier of
P
:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P(X\times C\times B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P(X\times C)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P(X\times C^{\prime}\times B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P(X\times C^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P(f\times\OPRid_{B})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\forall^{B}_{X\times C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\forall^{B}_{X\times C^{\prime}}}

and use it to prove the condition for
{\forall_{X,\varphi}}
:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P(X\times C\times B)_{\downarrow P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\varphi)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P(X\times C)_{\downarrow P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\varphi)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P(X\times C^{\prime}\times B)_{\downarrow P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\varphi)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P(X\times C^{\prime})_{\downarrow P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\varphi)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P(f\times\OPRid_{B})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\forall_{X,\varphi}}^{B}_{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\forall_{X,\varphi}}^{B}_{C^{\prime}}}

.

So take
\beta\in P(X\times C\times B)
:

\displaystyle P(f){\forall_{X,\varphi}}^{B}_{C}(\beta)

\displaystyle=P(f)(\forall^{B}_{X\times C}(\beta)\land P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\varphi))=P(f)\forall^{B}_{X\times C}(\beta)\land P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\varphi)

\displaystyle=\forall^{B}_{X\times C^{\prime}}P(f\times\OPRid)(\beta)\land P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\varphi)={\forall_{X,\varphi}}^{B}_{C^{\prime}}P(f\times\OPRid)(\beta).

If we ask in addition that the doctrine
P
satisfies Frobenius reciprocity for the adjunction
P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{2}\rangle)\dashv\forall^{B}_{X\times C}(\beta)
, then also the doctrine
P_{(X,\varphi)}
satisfies Frobenius for the adjunction
P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{2}\rangle)\dashv{\forall_{X,\varphi}}^{B}_{C}
: for any
\gamma\leq P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\varphi)
and
\beta\leq P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\varphi)
,

\displaystyle P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{2}\rangle)(\gamma\land{\forall_{X,\varphi}}^{B}_{C}(\beta))

\displaystyle=P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{2}\rangle)(\gamma\land{\forall}^{B}_{X\times C}(\beta)\land{P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\varphi)})

\displaystyle=P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{2}\rangle)(\gamma)\land\beta,

using
\gamma\land P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})\varphi=\gamma
and Frobenius reciprocity.

To conclude, consider the following diagram:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P(C\times B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P(X\times C\times B)_{\downarrow P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\varphi)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P(C)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P(X\times C)_{\downarrow P(s_{1})(\varphi)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{f}_{C\times B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\forall^{B}_{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\forall_{X,\varphi}}^{B}_{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{f}_{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{2}\rangle)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

We want to prove that the square with arrows pointing down and right is commutative. To do this, recall that the following diagram is commutative because of the Beck-Chevalley condition:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P(C\times B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P(C)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P(X\times C\times B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P(X\times C)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P(\mathrm{pr}_{2}\times\OPRid_{B})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\forall^{B}_{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P(\mathrm{pr}_{2})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\forall^{B}_{X\times C}}

So now take
\beta\in P(C\times B)
:

\displaystyle{\forall_{X,\varphi}}^{B}_{C}\mathfrak{f}_{C\times B}(\beta)

\displaystyle={\forall_{X,\varphi}}^{B}_{C}(P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\varphi)\land P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{2},\mathrm{pr}_{3}\rangle)(\beta))

\displaystyle=\forall^{B}_{X\times C}(P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\varphi)\land P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{2},\mathrm{pr}_{3}\rangle)(\beta))\land P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\varphi).

On the other hand

\mathfrak{f}_{C}\forall^{B}_{C}(\beta)=P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\varphi)\land P(\mathrm{pr}_{2})(\forall^{B}_{C}(\beta)).

To prove
{\forall_{X,\varphi}}^{B}_{C}\mathfrak{f}_{C\times B}(\beta)\leq\mathfrak{f}_{C}\forall^{B}_{C}(\beta)
, note that if holds if and only if

{\forall_{X,\varphi}}^{B}_{C}\mathfrak{f}_{C\times B}(\beta)\leq P(\mathrm{pr}_{2})\forall^{B}_{C}(\beta)

but
P(\mathrm{pr}_{2})\forall^{B}_{C}=\forall^{B}_{X\times C}P(\mathrm{pr}_{2}\times\OPRid)
and moreover
P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\varphi)\land P({\langle\mathrm{pr}_{2},\mathrm{pr}_{3}\rangle})(\beta)=P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\varphi)\land P({\mathrm{pr}_{2}\times\OPRid})(\beta)\leq P(\mathrm{pr}_{2}\times\OPRid)(\beta)
, so applying
\forall^{B}_{X\times C}
to both sides of the last inequality the claim follows.

Conversely, we have
\mathfrak{f}_{C}\forall^{B}_{C}(\beta)\leq{\forall_{X,\varphi}}^{B}_{C}\mathfrak{f}_{C\times B}(\beta)
, if and only if
P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{2}\rangle)\mathfrak{f}_{C}\forall^{B}_{C}(\beta)\leq\mathfrak{f}_{C\times B}(\beta)
but
P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{2}\rangle)=P_{(X,\varphi)}F_{X}(\mathrm{pr}_{1})
, so equivalently
\mathfrak{f}_{C\times B}P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})\forall^{B}_{C}(\beta)\leq\mathfrak{f}_{C\times B}(\beta)
. This proves the claim, by applying
\mathfrak{f}_{C\times B}
to
P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})\forall^{B}_{C}(\beta)\leq\beta
—which is the counit of the adjunction.∎

Implication

Proposition 5.7
.

Let
P:\mathbb{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
be a primary doctrine and
P_{(X,\varphi)}
be the Kleisli object of the comonad
(P,(X\times\mathrm{-},\mathfrak{f}),\gamma,\varepsilon)
defined by the pair
X\in\mathbb{C}
and
\varphi\in P(X)
. If
P
is an implicational doctrine, then
P_{(X,\varphi)}
is an implicational doctrine, and
(F_{X},\mathfrak{f})
is an implicational homomorphism.

Proof.

Since we already know that
P_{(X,\varphi)}
is primary, we check that
P_{(X,\varphi)}(A)=P(X\times A)_{\downarrow P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\varphi)}
is cartesian closed too: for any
\beta,\gamma\in P_{(X,\varphi)}(A)
, define
\beta\Rightarrow\gamma\coloneqq(\beta\to\gamma)\land P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\varphi)
.

This is indeed a natural transformation: take
f_{2}:A\rightsquigarrow B
, i.e.
f_{2}:X\times A\to B
, and write for convenience
f\coloneqq\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},f_{2}\rangle:X\times A\to X\times B
.
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{P(X\times B)_{\downarrow P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\varphi)}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times P(X\times B)_{\downarrow P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\varphi)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P(X\times B)_{\downarrow P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\varphi)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{P(X\times A)_{\downarrow P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\varphi)}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times P(X\times A)_{\downarrow P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\varphi)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P(X\times A)_{\downarrow P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\varphi)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P(f)\times P(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Rightarrow}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Rightarrow}

So, take a pair
\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}\in P(X\times B)_{\downarrow P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\varphi)}
: on the one hand it is sent to
P(f)(\alpha\Rightarrow\alpha^{\prime})=P(f)((\alpha\to\alpha^{\prime})\land P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\varphi))=(P(f)(\alpha)\to P(f)(\alpha^{\prime}))\land P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\varphi)
; on the other hand to
P(f)(\alpha)\Rightarrow P(f)(\alpha^{\prime})=(P(f)(\alpha)\to P(f)(\alpha^{\prime}))\land P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\varphi)
, so that
\Rightarrow
is indeed a natural transformation.

Now, to check that
P_{(X,\varphi)}(A)
endowed with this operation is cartesian closed, take three elements
\alpha,\beta,\gamma\in P(X\times A)_{\downarrow P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\varphi)}
, and we prove that
\alpha\land\beta\leq\gamma
if and only if
\alpha\leq\beta\Rightarrow\gamma
. So, suppose
\alpha\land\beta\leq\gamma
, then from
\alpha\leq\beta\to\gamma
combined with the assumption on
\alpha
we obtain
\alpha\leq(\beta\to\gamma)\land P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\gamma)
. Conversely, from
\alpha\leq(\beta\to\gamma)\land P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\gamma)\leq\beta\to\gamma
, it follows that
\alpha\land\beta\leq\gamma
.

Take
\mathfrak{f}_{A}:P(A)\to P_{(X,\varphi)}(A)=P(X\times A)_{\downarrow P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\varphi)}
. For any
\alpha,\beta\in P(A)
, one has on the one side
\mathfrak{f}_{A}(\alpha\to\beta)=P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\varphi)\land P(\mathrm{pr}_{2})(\alpha\to\beta)
, and on the other hand
\mathfrak{f}_{A}(\alpha)\Rightarrow\mathfrak{f}_{A}(\beta)=(P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\varphi)\land P(\mathrm{pr}_{2})(\alpha))\Rightarrow(P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\varphi)\land P(\mathrm{pr}_{2})(\beta))
. So now we prove that in any cartesian closed poset,

((x\land a)\to(x\land b))\land x=x\land(a\to b)

First of all,
x\land(a\to b)\leq((x\land a)\to(x\land b))\land x
if and only if
x\land(a\to b)\leq(x\land a)\to(x\land b)
if and only if
x\land{(a\to b)}\land x\land{a}\leq x\land b
.

Conversely,
((x\land a)\to(x\land b))\land x\leq x\land(a\to b)
if and only if
((x\land a)\to(x\land b))\land x\leq a\to b
if and only if
((x\land a)\to(x\land b))\land x\land a\leq b
, but
((x\land a)\to(x\land b))\land x\land a\leq x\land b\leq b
.∎

Finite joins

Proposition 5.8
.

Let
P:\mathbb{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
be a primary doctrine and
P_{(X,\varphi)}
be the Kleisli object of the comonad
(P,(X\times\mathrm{-},\mathfrak{f}),\gamma,\varepsilon)
defined by the pair
X\in\mathbb{C}
and
\varphi\in P(X)
. If
P
is bounded, then
P_{(X,\varphi)}
is bounded, and
(F_{X},\mathfrak{f})
preserves the bottom element.

Proof.

The poset
P_{(X,\varphi)}(A)
has bottom element, which is
\mathsf{0}_{A}\coloneqq\bot_{X\times A}
, and
\mathsf{0}
is natural.

Take
\mathfrak{f}_{A}:P(A)\to P_{(X,\varphi)}(A)=P(X\times A)_{\downarrow P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\varphi)}
. Compute
\mathfrak{f}_{A}(\bot_{A})=P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\varphi)\land{P(\mathrm{pr}_{2})(\bot_{A})}=\bot_{X\times A}=\mathsf{0}_{A}
, so the bottom element is preserved.∎

Proposition 5.9
.

Let
P:\mathbb{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
be a primary doctrine and
P_{(X,\varphi)}
be the Kleisli object of the comonad
(P,(X\times\mathrm{-},\mathfrak{f}),\gamma,\varepsilon)
defined by the pair
X\in\mathbb{C}
and
\varphi\in P(X)
. If
P
has binary joins, then
P_{(X,\varphi)}
has binary joins. If each fiber of
P
is a distributive lattice, then
(F_{X},\mathfrak{f})
preserves binary joins.

Proof.

To check that
P_{(X,\varphi)}(A)=P(X\times A)_{\downarrow P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\varphi)}
has binary joins, take any two elements
\alpha,\beta\in P_{(X,\varphi)}(A)
, and compute their join
\alpha\lor\beta
in
P(X\times A)
. Naturality of the operation is trivial.

Now, for any two elements
\alpha,\beta\in P(A)
, one has
\mathfrak{f}_{A}(\alpha\lor\beta)=P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\varphi)\land P(\mathrm{pr}_{2})(\alpha\lor\beta)=P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\varphi)\land(P(\mathrm{pr}_{2})(\alpha)\lor P(\mathrm{pr}_{2})(\beta))

On the other hand,
\mathfrak{f}_{A}(\alpha)\lor\mathfrak{f}_{A}(\beta)=(P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\varphi)\land P(\mathrm{pr}_{2})(\alpha))\lor(P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\varphi)\land P(\mathrm{pr}_{2})(\beta))
; in general this is not equal to
\mathfrak{f}_{A}(\alpha\lor\beta)
, computed above.
However, the equality holds if we ask for
P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\varphi)\land(\mathrm{-})
to preserve joins, e.g. whenever the lattice is distributive.∎

Corollary 5.10
.

Let
P:\mathbb{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
be a primary doctrine and
P_{(X,\varphi)}
be the Kleisli object of the comonad
(P,(X\times\mathrm{-},\mathfrak{f}),\gamma,\varepsilon)
defined by the pair
X\in\mathbb{C}
and
\varphi\in P(X)
. If
P
is a Heyting doctrine, then
P_{(X,\varphi)}
is a Heyting doctrine, and
(F_{X},\mathfrak{f})
is a Heyting homomorphism.

Proof.

Finite meets, finite joins and implication are preserved by the construction.∎

Booleanness

Proposition 5.11
.

Let
P:\mathbb{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
be a primary doctrine and
P_{(X,\varphi)}
be the Kleisli object of the comonad
(P,(X\times\mathrm{-},\mathfrak{f}),\gamma,\varepsilon)
defined by the pair
X\in\mathbb{C}
and
\varphi\in P(X)
. If
P
is a Boolean doctrine, then
P_{(X,\varphi)}
is a Boolean doctrine, and
(F_{X},\mathfrak{f})
is a Boolean homomorphism.

Proof.

We want to check that
P_{(X,\varphi)}(A)=P(X\times A)_{\downarrow P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\varphi)}
is a boolean algebra: for any element
\alpha\in P_{(X,\varphi)}(A)
, define
\rceil\alpha\coloneqq\alpha\Rightarrow\bot=(\alpha\to\bot)\land P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\varphi)=\lnot\alpha\land P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\varphi)
. Since we already know that
P_{(X,\varphi)}(A)
is a Heyting algebra, we only have to prove that
\rceil\rceil\alpha=\alpha
:

\rceil\rceil\alpha=\rceil(\lnot\alpha\land P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\varphi))=\lnot(\lnot\alpha\land P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\varphi))\land P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\varphi)\\
=(\lnot\lnot\alpha\lor\lnot P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\varphi))\land P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\varphi)=(\alpha\lor P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\lnot\varphi))\land P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\varphi)\\
=(\alpha\land{P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\varphi)})\lor{P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})({\lnot\varphi\land\varphi})}=\alpha\lor\bot=\alpha.

To conclude,
(F_{X},\mathfrak{f})
is Boolean since the Heyting structure is preserved by
\mathfrak{f}
.∎

Variations on negation

There are other ways to introduce negation in the context of inf-semilattices. Here we describe two examples and check that properties are again preserved.

Definition 5.12
.

A primary doctrine
P:\mathbb{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
is
*
-autonomous
if for every object
A
, the poset
P(A)
is
*
-autonomous, that is:
P(A)
is cartesian, endowed with operation
\lnot
such that
\lnot\lnot a=a
for every
a\in P(A)
and such that
a\land b\leq\lnot c
if and only if
a\leq\lnot(b\land c)
. Moreover the operation
\lnot:P^{\mathrm{op}}\to P
yields a natural transformation.

A primary doctrine homomorphism between two
*
-autonomous doctrines is
*
-autonomous
if it preserves the negation.

Proposition 5.13
.

Let
P:\mathbb{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
be a primary doctrine and
P_{(X,\varphi)}
be the Kleisli object of the comonad
(P,(X\times\mathrm{-},\mathfrak{f}),\gamma,\varepsilon)
defined by the pair
X\in\mathbb{C}
and
\varphi\in P(X)
. If
P
is a
*
-autonomous doctrine, then
P_{(X,\varphi)}
is a
*
-autonomous doctrine, and
(F_{X},\mathfrak{f})
is a
*
-autonomous homomorphism.

Proof.

For any
\alpha\in P_{(X,\varphi)}(A)=P(X\times A)_{\downarrow P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\varphi)}
, define
\rceil\alpha\coloneqq\lnot\alpha\land P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\varphi)
. The operation extended on each fiber
\rceil:{P_{(X,\varphi)}}^{\mathrm{op}}\to{P_{(X,\varphi)}}
is trivially a natural transformation. We call
x=P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\varphi)
for simplicity, and we prove
\rceil\rceil\alpha=\alpha
.

\alpha\leq\rceil\rceil\alpha\text{ if and only if }\alpha\leq\rceil(\lnot\alpha\land\ x)\text{ if and only if }\alpha\leq\lnot(\lnot\alpha\land\ x)\land x\\
\text{ if and only if }{\alpha}\leq\lnot({\lnot\alpha}\land{x})\text{ if and only if }\alpha\land x\leq\lnot\lnot\alpha=\alpha.

Conversely,

\rceil\rceil\alpha\leq\alpha\text{ if and only if }{\lnot(\lnot\alpha\land\ x)}\land{x}\leq\lnot{\lnot\alpha}\text{ if and only if }\lnot(\lnot\alpha\land\ x)\leq\lnot(x\land\lnot\alpha).

Now, to prove the equivalence, take
\alpha,\beta,\gamma\leq x
, then

\alpha\land\beta\leq\rceil\gamma\text{ if and only if }\alpha\land\beta\leq\lnot\gamma\land x\text{ if and only if }\alpha\land\beta\leq\lnot\gamma\\
\text{ if and only if }\alpha\leq\lnot(\beta\land\gamma)\text{ if and only if }\alpha\leq\lnot(\beta\land\gamma)\land x=\rceil(\beta\land\gamma).

To conclude, we prove that
\mathfrak{f}_{A}:P(A)\to P_{(X,\varphi)}(A)
preserves the negation. On the one hand
\mathfrak{f}_{A}(\lnot\alpha)=P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\varphi)\land P(\mathrm{pr}_{2})(\lnot\alpha)=P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\varphi)\land\lnot P(\mathrm{pr}_{2})(\alpha)
and, on the other hand
\rceil\mathfrak{f}_{A}(\alpha)=\lnot\mathfrak{f}_{A}(\alpha)\land P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\varphi)=\lnot(P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\varphi)\land P(\mathrm{pr}_{2})(\alpha))\land P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\varphi)
. To see this, it is enough to check that in a
*
-autonomous inf-semilattice we have
x\land\lnot a=\lnot(x\land a)\land x
, for any
a,x
.
First of all,

x\land\lnot a\leq\lnot(x\land a)\land x\text{ if and only if }x\land\lnot a\leq\lnot(x\land a)\text{ if and only if }x\land\lnot a\land x\leq\lnot a;

conversely,

\lnot(x\land a)\land x\leq x\land\lnot a\text{ if and only if }\lnot(x\land a)\land x\leq\lnot a\text{ if and only if }\lnot(x\land a)\leq\lnot(x\land a).\qed

Definition 5.14
.

A primary doctrine
P:\mathbb{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}

has pseudo-complements
if for every object
A
, the poset
P(A)
has pseudo-complements, that is: the poset
P(A)
is cartesian, endowed with an operation
\lnot
and a bottom element
\bot
, where
\lnot a=\text{max}\{b\mid a\land b=\bot\}
; moreover the operations
\bot:\mathbf{1}\to P
,
\lnot:P^{\mathrm{op}}\to P
yield natural transformations .

A primary doctrine homomorphism between two doctrines with pseudo-complements
preserves pseudo-complements
if it is bounded and preserves the negation.

Proposition 5.15
.

Let
P:\mathbb{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
be a primary doctrine and
P_{(X,\varphi)}
be the Kleisli object of the comonad
(P,(X\times\mathrm{-},\mathfrak{f}),\gamma,\varepsilon)
defined by the pair
X\in\mathbb{C}
and
\varphi\in P(X)
. If
P
has pseudo-complements, then
P_{(X,\varphi)}
has pseudo-complements, and
(F_{X},\mathfrak{f})
preserves pseudo-complements.

Proof.

For any
\alpha\in P_{(X,\varphi)}(A)=P(X\times A)_{\downarrow P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\varphi)}
, define
\rceil\alpha\coloneqq\lnot\alpha\land P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\varphi)
; clearly
\rceil:{P_{(X,\varphi)}}^{\mathrm{op}}\to{P_{(X,\varphi)}}
is a natural transformation. First of all we observe that
\alpha\land\rceil\alpha=\bot
, since
\alpha\land\lnot\alpha\land P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\varphi)=\bot
; then, suppose
\beta\leq P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\varphi)
such that
\alpha\land\beta=\bot
, but from
\alpha\land\beta=\bot
,
\beta\leq\lnot\alpha
follows in
P(X\times A)
, so
\beta\leq\lnot\alpha\land P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\varphi)=\rceil\alpha
, hence
\rceil\alpha=\text{max}\{\beta\mid\alpha\land\beta=\bot\}
.

To conclude, we prove that
\mathfrak{f}_{A}:P(A)\to P_{(X,\varphi)}(A)
preserves the negation. Take
\mathfrak{f}_{A}(\lnot\alpha)
and
\rceil\mathfrak{f}_{A}(\alpha)
computed as above—in the
*
-autonomous case—, so again we check that
x\land\lnot a=\lnot(x\land a)\land x
for any
a,x
in a pseudo-complemented poset. First of all,

x\land\lnot a\leq\lnot(x\land a)\land x\text{ iff }x\land\lnot a\leq\lnot(x\land a)\text{, so it is sufficient }(x\land\lnot a)\land(x\land a)=\bot.

Conversely,

\lnot(x\land a)\land x\leq x\land\lnot a\text{ iff }\lnot(x\land a)\land x\leq\lnot a\text{, so it is sufficient }(\lnot(x\land a)\land x)\land a=\bot.

∎

Weak Power Objects

Recall from Definition 4.9 in
[
Pas15
]
that a doctrine
P
has weak power objects if for every object
A
in the base category
\mathbb{C}
, there exists an object
\mathsf{P}(A)
and an element
\boldsymbol{\in}_{A}\in P(A\times\mathsf{P}(A))
such that for any object
B
and
\phi\in P(A\times B)
there exists an arrow
\{\phi\}:B\to\mathsf{P}(A)
such that
\phi=P(\OPRid_{A}\times\{\phi\})(\boldsymbol{\in}_{A})
.

Proposition 5.16
.

Let
P:\mathbb{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
be a primary doctrine and
P_{(X,\varphi)}
be the Kleisli object of the comonad
(P,(X\times\mathrm{-},\mathfrak{f}),\gamma,\varepsilon)
defined by the pair
X\in\mathbb{C}
and
\varphi\in P(X)
. If
P
has weak power objects, then
P_{(X,\varphi)}
has weak power objects.

Proof.

Since
\mathbb{C}_{X}
has the same objects as
\mathbb{C}
, for any object
A
consider
\mathsf{P}(A)
and the element
\mathfrak{f}_{A\times\mathsf{P}(A)}(\boldsymbol{\in}_{A})\in P_{(X,\varphi)}(A\times\mathsf{P}(A))
, i.e.
P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})\varphi\land P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{2},\mathrm{pr}_{3}\rangle)(\boldsymbol{\in}_{A})\in P(X\times A\times\mathsf{P}(A))_{\downarrow P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})\varphi}
. We want to prove that
(\mathsf{P}(A),\mathfrak{f}_{A\times\mathsf{P}(A)}(\boldsymbol{\in}_{A}))
is a weak power object of
A
in the doctrine
P_{(X,\varphi)}
.

To see this, we take any object
C
and
\psi\in P_{(X,\varphi)}(A\times C)=P(X\times A\times C)_{\downarrow P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})\varphi}
and look for an arrow
[\psi]:C\rightsquigarrow\mathsf{P}(A)
—i.e. a
\mathbb{C}
-arrow
X\times C\to\mathsf{P}(A)
—such that

\psi=P_{(X,\varphi)}(\OPRid_{A}\times_{\mathbb{C}_{X}}[\psi])\mathfrak{f}_{A\times\mathsf{P}(A)}(\boldsymbol{\in}_{A}).

Here, the product
\OPRid_{A}\times_{\mathbb{C}_{X}}[\psi]
is computed in
\mathbb{C}_{X}
, hence it is actually the
\mathbb{C}
-arrow

\langle\mathrm{pr}_{2},[\psi]\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{3}\rangle\rangle:X\times A\times C\to A\times\mathsf{P}(A).

Using the fact that
P
has weak power object, we can take the object
X\times C
and the element
P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{2},\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{3}\rangle)\psi\in P(A\times X\times C)
and we know that there exists

[\psi]\coloneqq\{P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{2},\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{3}\rangle)\psi\}:X\times C\to\mathsf{P}(A)

such that

P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{2},\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{3}\rangle)\psi=P\left(\OPRid_{A}\times\{P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{2},\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{3}\rangle)\psi\}\right)(\boldsymbol{\in}_{A})=P\left(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},[\psi]\langle\mathrm{pr}_{2},\mathrm{pr}_{3}\rangle\rangle\right)(\boldsymbol{\in}_{A}).

Now compute

\displaystyle P_{(X,\varphi)}(\OPRid_{A}\times_{\mathbb{C}_{X}}[\psi])\mathfrak{f}_{A\times\mathsf{P}(A)}(\boldsymbol{\in}_{A})

\displaystyle=P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{2},[\psi]\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{3}\rangle\rangle)\big(P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})\varphi\land P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{2},\mathrm{pr}_{3}\rangle)(\boldsymbol{\in}_{A})\big)

\displaystyle=P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})\varphi\land P\left(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{2},[\psi]\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{3}\rangle\rangle\right)(\boldsymbol{\in}_{A})

which is equal to
\psi
if and only if
P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{2},\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{3}\rangle)\psi=P(\mathrm{pr}_{2})\varphi\land P\left(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},[\psi]\langle\mathrm{pr}_{2},\mathrm{pr}_{3}\rangle\rangle\right)(\boldsymbol{\in}_{A})
, but this is true following from the definition of
[\psi]
and the fact that
\psi\leq P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})\varphi
.∎

6
Universal properties of
P_{(X,\varphi)}

Consider the following diagram for a primary doctrine
P
. In particular,
P_{(X,\varphi)}
is primary too.
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}^{\text{op}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{C}_{X}}^{\text{op}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{Pos}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{X}}^{\text{op}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{(X,\varphi)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}

We can interpret this 1-arrow as follows: we are adding a constant of sort
X
to the theory
P
, and making this constant verify
\varphi
.
Indeed, take
\mathfrak{f}_{X}(\varphi)\in P_{(X,\varphi)}(X)
, which is the interpretation of
\varphi
in
P_{(X,\varphi)}
, and consider the constant
\mathrm{pr}_{1}:\mathbf{t}\rightsquigarrow X
in
\mathbb{C}_{X}
.

Notation 6.1
.

When there is no confusion, the terminal object of a given category will be simply called
\mathbf{t}
. Otherwise, a subscript will specify the category in which we are computing the terminal object.

This map is the
\mathbb{C}
-arrow
\mathrm{pr}_{1}:X\times\mathbf{t}\to X
, which is a direction of the canonical isomorphism
X\times\mathbf{t}\cong X
, whose inverse is given by
\langle\OPRid_{X},!_{X}\rangle:X\to X\times\mathbf{t}
. This induces an isomorphism also between the corresponding fibers
P(X\times\mathbf{t})\cong P(X)
, so that
P_{(X,\varphi)}(\mathbf{t})=P(X\times\mathbf{t})_{\downarrow P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})\varphi}\cong P(X)_{\downarrow\varphi}
. From now on we will write
\OPRid_{X}:\mathbf{t}\rightsquigarrow X
instead of
\mathrm{pr}_{1}:\mathbf{t}\rightsquigarrow X
, and
P(X)_{\downarrow\varphi}
instead of
P(X\times\mathbf{t})_{\downarrow P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})\varphi}
. With this notation, we compute the reindexing of
\mathfrak{f}_{X}(\varphi)
along the constant
\OPRid_{X}:\mathbf{t}\rightsquigarrow X
, and we show that it is the top element in
P_{(X,\varphi)}(\mathbf{t})=P(X)_{\downarrow\varphi}
. Indeed:

P_{(X,\varphi)}(\OPRid_{X})\mathfrak{f}_{X}(\varphi)=P(\Delta_{X})\mathfrak{f}_{X}(\varphi)=P(\Delta_{X})(P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})\varphi\land P(\mathrm{pr}_{2})\varphi)=\varphi\land\varphi=\varphi,

and
\varphi
is the top element of
P_{(X,\varphi)}(\mathbf{t})=P(X)_{\downarrow\varphi}
, which means, the interpretation of
\varphi
evaluated in the new constant is true.

Theorem 6.2
.

Let
P:\mathbb{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
be a primary doctrine. Given an object
X
in the base category
\mathbb{C}
and an element
\varphi\in P(X)
, the 1-arrow
(F_{X},\mathfrak{f}):P\to P_{(X,\varphi)}
and the
\mathbb{C}_{X}
-arrow
\OPRid_{X}:\mathbf{t}_{\mathbb{C}_{X}}\rightsquigarrow X
are such that
\top\leq P_{(X,\varphi)}(\OPRid_{X})\mathfrak{f}_{X}(\varphi)
in
P_{(X,\varphi)}(\mathbf{t}_{\mathbb{C}_{X}})
, and they are universal with respect to this property, i.e. for any primary 1-arrow
(G,\mathfrak{g}):P\to R
, where
R:\mathbb{D}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
is a primary doctrine, and any
\mathbb{D}
-arrow
c:\mathbf{t}_{\mathbb{D}}\to G(X)
such that
\top\leq R(c)\mathfrak{g}_{X}(\varphi)
in
R(\mathbf{t}_{\mathbb{D}})
there exists a unique up to a unique natural isomorphism primary 1-arrow
(G^{\prime},\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}):P_{(X,\varphi)}\to R
such that
(G^{\prime},\mathfrak{g}^{\prime})\circ(F_{X},\mathfrak{f})=(G,\mathfrak{g})
and
G^{\prime}(\OPRid_{X})=c
.

Proof.

Consider the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(P,(X\times\mathrm{-},\mathfrak{f}),\gamma,\varepsilon)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(P_{(X,\varphi)},(\OPRid,\OPRid),\OPRid,\OPRid)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(R,(\OPRid,\OPRid),\OPRid,\OPRid)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces((G,\mathfrak{g}),\mathfrak{j})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces((F_{X},\mathfrak{f}),\gamma)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces((G^{\prime},\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}),\OPRid)}

(6.1)

describing the universal property for the Kleisli construction for the comonad we are studying on
P
—see diagram (
2.1
) in Proposition
2.19
.

So, in order to construct
(G^{\prime},\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}):P_{(X,\varphi)}\to R
, we must define
\mathfrak{j}
in such a way that
((G,\mathfrak{g}),\mathfrak{j})
is an arrow in
\Cmd^{*}(\mathbf{IdxPos})
as in (
6.1
), i.e. a natural transformation
\mathfrak{j}:G\xrightarrow{\cdot}G(X\times\mathrm{-})
, such that
\mathfrak{g}_{A}\leq R(\mathfrak{j}_{A})\mathfrak{g}_{X\times A}\mathfrak{f}_{A}
and satisfying the coherence diagrams.
Knowing that
G
preserves products, we need to define for every object
A
an arrow
\mathfrak{j}_{A}:GA\to GX\times GA
take
\mathfrak{j}_{A}\coloneqq\langle c\cdot!_{GA},\OPRid_{GA}\rangle
, where
!_{GA}:GA\to\mathbf{t}_{\mathbb{D}}
is the unique arrow from
GA
to the teminal object.

This is a natural transformation:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces GA}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces GX\times GA}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces GB}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces GX\times GB}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{j}_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G(\OPRid\times f)=\OPRid\times G(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{j}_{B}}

.

Indeed, for any
f:A\to B
, we have:

(\OPRid\times G(f))\langle c\cdot!_{GA},\OPRid_{GA}\rangle=\langle c\cdot!_{GA},G(f)\rangle=\langle c\cdot!_{GB},\OPRid_{GB}\rangle G(f).

Moreover, for any
\alpha\in P(A)
, we have

\displaystyle R(\mathfrak{j}_{A})\mathfrak{g}_{X\times A}\mathfrak{f}_{A}(\alpha)

\displaystyle=R(\mathfrak{j}_{A})\mathfrak{g}_{X\times A}(P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\varphi)\land P(\mathrm{pr}_{2})(\alpha))

\displaystyle=R(\mathfrak{j}_{A})\big(R{G(\mathrm{pr}_{1})}\mathfrak{g}_{X}(\varphi)\land R{G(\mathrm{pr}_{2})}\mathfrak{g}_{A}(\alpha)\big)=R(c\cdot!_{GA})\mathfrak{g}_{X}(\varphi)\land\mathfrak{g}_{A}(\alpha),

using naturality of
\mathfrak{g}
and the fact that
G
preserves products.

So now observe that
\mathfrak{g}_{A}(\alpha)\leq R(c\cdot!_{GA})\mathfrak{g}_{X}(\varphi)\land\mathfrak{g}_{A}(\alpha)
if and only if
\mathfrak{g}_{A}(\alpha)\leq R(c\cdot!_{GA})\mathfrak{g}_{X}(\varphi)
, but by assumption
R(c)\mathfrak{g}_{X}(\varphi)=\top
in
R(\mathbf{t}_{\mathbb{D}})
, so
R(!_{GA})R(c)\mathfrak{g}_{X}(\varphi)=R(c\cdot!_{GA})\mathfrak{g}_{X}(\varphi)=\top
in
R(GA)
, hence the inequality holds.

To conclude, we prove that the coherence diagrams commute:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces GX\times G\mathrm{-}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces GX\times G\mathrm{-}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces GX\times G\mathrm{-}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces GX\times GX\times G\mathrm{-}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G(\gamma)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\OPRid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G(\varepsilon)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{j}_{X\times\mathrm{-}}}

.

The first diagram commutes since

\displaystyle G(\Delta_{X}\times\OPRid_{A})\mathfrak{j}_{A}

\displaystyle=(\Delta_{GX}\times\OPRid_{GA})\langle c\cdot!_{GA},\OPRid_{GA}\rangle=\langle c\cdot!_{GA},c\cdot!_{GA},\OPRid_{GA}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle c\cdot!_{G(X\times A)},\OPRid_{G(X\times A)}\rangle\langle c\cdot!_{GA},\OPRid_{GA}\rangle,

while the second one commutes since

G(\varepsilon_{A})\mathfrak{j}_{A}=G(\mathrm{pr}_{2})\mathfrak{j}_{A}=\mathrm{pr}_{2}^{\prime}\mathfrak{j}_{A}=\OPRid_{GA}.

So now we know from the universal property that there exists a unique
(G^{\prime},\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}):P_{(X,\varphi)}\to R
such that
((G,\mathfrak{g}),\mathfrak{j})=((G^{\prime},\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}),\OPRid)((F_{K},\mathfrak{f}),\gamma)
. In particular there is an arrow
(G^{\prime},\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}):P_{(X,\varphi)}\to R
such that
(G^{\prime},\mathfrak{g}^{\prime})\circ(F_{K},\mathfrak{f})=(G,\mathfrak{g})
. Moreover, if we translate the universal property in Proposition
2.19
to the notation used here, we observe that
G^{\prime}(g:A\rightsquigarrow B)=G(g)\mathfrak{j}_{A}
, where
g:X\times A\to B
is an arrow in
\mathbb{C}
. In particular, taking
A=\mathbf{t}_{\mathbb{C}_{X}}
and
g=\OPRid_{X}:X\to X
, we obtain
G^{\prime}(\OPRid_{X})=\mathfrak{j}_{\mathbf{t}_{\mathbb{C}}}=c
. Here we use the fact that
G
preserves the terminal object, and that the product of an object with the terminal object is the object itself.

We use the definition of
G^{\prime}
on arrows to prove that
G^{\prime}
preserves products; consider the following three diagrams: on the left there is the
\mathbb{C}
-diagram that mirrors a product diagram in
\mathbb{C}_{X}
—in the middle—, while on the right there is the image of such product through
G^{\prime}
.
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times A\times B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\times B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces GA\times GB}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces GA}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces GB}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{pr}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{pr}_{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{pr}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{pr}_{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G(\mathrm{pr}_{2})\mathfrak{j}_{A\times B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G(\mathrm{pr}_{3})\mathfrak{j}_{A\times B}}

However, since
G
preserves products,
G(\mathrm{pr}_{2})
and
G(\mathrm{pr}_{3})
are respectively the second and third projections from
GX\times GA\times GB
, and these precomposed with
\mathfrak{j}_{A\times B}
are precisely the first and second projections from
GA\times GB
, as claimed.

To show that
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}
preserves infima and top element, recall from Proposition
2.19
that
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}_{A}
is the restriction of
R(\mathfrak{j}_{A})\mathfrak{g}_{X\times A}
. Then notice that for any
\alpha,\beta\in P_{(X,\varphi)}(A)
we have

\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}_{A}(\alpha\land\beta)=R(\mathfrak{j}_{A})\mathfrak{g}_{X\times A}(\alpha\land\beta)=R(\mathfrak{j}_{A})\big(\mathfrak{g}_{X\times A}(\alpha)\land\mathfrak{g}_{X\times A}(\beta)\big)=\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}_{A}(\alpha)\land\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}_{A}(\beta),

since by assumption
\mathfrak{g}
respect the structures, while for the top element:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}_{A}(P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})\varphi)

\displaystyle=R(\mathfrak{j}_{A})\mathfrak{g}_{X\times A}P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})\varphi=R(\langle c\cdot!_{GA},\OPRid_{GA}\rangle)R(\mathrm{pr}_{1})\mathfrak{g}_{X}\varphi

\displaystyle=R(!_{GA})R(c)\mathfrak{g}_{X}\varphi=\top_{GA}.

So
(G^{\prime},\mathfrak{g}^{\prime})
is indeed a primary 1-arrow.

Finally, suppose that
(\overline{G},\overline{\mathfrak{g}}):P_{(X,\varphi)}\to R
is another primary 1-arrow such that
(\overline{G},\overline{\mathfrak{g}})\circ(F_{X},\mathfrak{f})=(G,\mathfrak{g})
and
\overline{G}(\OPRid_{X})=c
.

Then we can compute the composition
((G,\mathfrak{g}),\overline{\mathfrak{j}})=((\overline{G},\overline{\mathfrak{g}}),\OPRid)((F_{K},\mathfrak{f}),\gamma)
, where we define
\overline{\mathfrak{j}}_{A}:=(\OPRid\circ\gamma)_{A}=\overline{G}(\varepsilon_{X\times A}\gamma_{A})=\overline{G}(\OPRid_{X\times A})
—see Remark
2.20
and Proposition
2.19
. We claim that
\overline{\mathfrak{j}}=\mathfrak{j}
, so that by uniqueness given by the universal property, the equality
(G^{\prime},\mathfrak{g}^{\prime})=(\overline{G},\overline{\mathfrak{g}})
follows.

In our notation, we have to think of
\OPRid_{X\times A}
as the map
A\rightsquigarrow X\times A
, and it is uniquely defined by its two components: the first one is
\mathrm{pr}_{1}:A\rightsquigarrow X
, the second one is
\mathrm{pr}_{2}:A\rightsquigarrow A
. Observe that
\mathrm{pr}_{2}
is the identity of
A
in
\mathbb{C}_{X}
, while
\mathrm{pr}_{1}:A\rightsquigarrow X
is the composition of the unique arrow
A\rightsquigarrow\mathbf{t}_{\mathbb{C}}
and the constant
\OPRid_{X}:\mathbf{t}_{\mathbb{C}_{X}}\rightsquigarrow X
. Since
\overline{G}
preserves products,
\overline{\mathfrak{j}}_{A}=\overline{G}(\OPRid_{X\times A}):GA\to GX\times GA
must be the identity of
GA
on the second component; in particular the second components of
\overline{\mathfrak{j}}_{A}
and
\mathfrak{j}_{A}
are the same. Concerning the first component we have
\overline{G}(\mathrm{pr}_{1})=\overline{G}(\OPRid_{X})\overline{G}(!_{X\times A})=c\cdot!_{GA}
, i.e. also the first component of
\overline{\mathfrak{j}}_{A}
coincides with the first component of
\mathfrak{j}_{A}
, hence the two maps coincide as claimed.∎

Theorem 6.3
.

Let
P
,
P_{(X,\varphi)}
,
R
,
(G,\mathfrak{g}):P\to R
be the doctrines and a morphism with the same assumption of Theorem
6.2
. Then

(i)

if
P,R
and
(G,\mathfrak{g})
are elementary, then
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}
preserves the fibered equality;

(ii)

if
P,R
and
(G,\mathfrak{g})
are existential, then
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}
preserves the existential quantifier;

(iii)

if
P,R
and
(G,\mathfrak{g})
are universal, then
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}
preserves the universal quantifier;

(iv)

if
P,R
and
(G,\mathfrak{g})
are implicational, then
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}
preserves the implication;

(v)

if
P,R
are bounded, with top and bottom elements preserved by
\mathfrak{g}
, then
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}
preserves them;

(vi)

if
P,R
have binary joins, preserved by
\mathfrak{g}
, then
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}
preserves binary joins;

(vii)

if
P,R
and
(G,\mathfrak{g})
are respectively Heyting or Boolean, then
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}
preserves the corresponding structure.

Proof.

(i)

We need to check that
\mathfrak{g^{\prime}}_{A\times A}(\delta^{X,\varphi})_{A}=\delta_{GA}
.

\displaystyle\mathfrak{g^{\prime}}_{A\times A}(\delta^{X,\varphi})_{A}

\displaystyle=R(\mathfrak{j}_{A\times A})\mathfrak{g}_{X\times A\times A}\big(P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\varphi)\land P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{2},\mathrm{pr}_{3}\rangle)(\delta_{A})\big)

\displaystyle=R(\mathfrak{j}_{A\times A})R(\mathrm{pr}_{1})\mathfrak{g}_{X}(\varphi)\land R(\langle c!_{GA\times GA},\mathrm{pr}_{2},\mathrm{pr}_{3}\rangle)R(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{2},\mathrm{pr}_{3}\rangle)\mathfrak{g}_{A\times A}(\delta_{A})

\displaystyle=R(!_{GA\times GA})R(c)\mathfrak{g}_{X}(\varphi)\land\mathfrak{g}_{A\times A}(\delta_{A})=\top_{GA\times GA}\land\delta_{GA}=\delta_{GA}.

(ii)

We prove that
\exists^{GB}_{GC}\mathfrak{g^{\prime}}_{C\times B}=\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}_{C}{\exists_{X,\varphi}}^{B}_{C}
:

\displaystyle\exists^{GB}_{GC}\mathfrak{g^{\prime}}_{C\times B}

\displaystyle=\exists^{GB}_{GC}R({\mathfrak{j}_{C\times B}})\mathfrak{g}_{X\times C\times B}=R(\mathfrak{j}_{C})\exists^{GB}_{GX\times GC}\mathfrak{g}_{X\times C\times B}

\displaystyle=R(\mathfrak{j}_{C})\mathfrak{g}_{X\times C}\exists^{B}_{X\times C}=\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}_{C}{\exists_{X,\varphi}}^{B}_{C}.

(iii)

The proof is similar to the one above, with a little alteration:

\displaystyle\forall^{GB}_{GC}\mathfrak{g^{\prime}}_{C\times B}

\displaystyle=\forall^{GB}_{GC}R(\mathfrak{j}_{C\times B})\mathfrak{g}_{X\times C\times B}

\displaystyle=R(\mathfrak{j}_{C})\forall^{GB}_{GX\times GC}\mathfrak{g}_{X\times C\times B}=R(\mathfrak{j}_{C})\mathfrak{g}_{X\times C}\forall^{B}_{X\times C}

and also

\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}_{C}{\forall_{X,\varphi}}^{B}_{C}

\displaystyle=R(\mathfrak{j}_{C})\mathfrak{g}_{X\times C}\big(\forall^{B}_{X\times C}(\mathrm{-})\land P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})\varphi\big)

\displaystyle=R(\mathfrak{j}_{C})\mathfrak{g}_{X\times C}\forall^{B}_{X\times C}(\mathrm{-})\land{R(\mathfrak{j}_{C})\mathfrak{g}_{X\times C}P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})\varphi}=R(\mathfrak{j}_{C})\mathfrak{g}_{X\times C}\forall^{B}_{X\times C}.

Note that
{R(\mathfrak{j}_{C})\mathfrak{g}_{X\times C}P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})\varphi}=\top_{GC}
since
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}
preserves the top element—see Theorem
6.2
.

(iv)

Take
\alpha,\beta\in P_{(X,\varphi)}(A)
:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}_{A}(\alpha\Rightarrow\beta)

\displaystyle=R(\mathfrak{j}_{A})\mathfrak{g}_{X\times A}((\alpha\rightarrow\beta)\land P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})\varphi)

\displaystyle=\big(R(\mathfrak{j}_{A})\mathfrak{g}_{X\times A}(\alpha)\rightarrow R(\mathfrak{j}_{A})\mathfrak{g}_{X\times A}(\beta)\big)\land{R(\mathfrak{j}_{A})\mathfrak{g}_{X\times A}P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})\varphi}=\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}_{A}(\alpha)\rightarrow\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}_{A}(\beta).

(v)

Compute
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}_{A}(\mathsf{0}_{A})=R(\mathfrak{j}_{A})\mathfrak{g}_{X\times A}(\bot_{A})=\bot_{GA}
.

(vi)

Take
\alpha,\beta\in P_{(X,\varphi)}(A)
, then

\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}_{A}(\alpha\lor\beta)=R(\mathfrak{j}_{A})\mathfrak{g}_{X\times A}(\alpha\lor\beta)=\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}_{A}(\alpha)\lor\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}_{A}(\beta).

Observe that in order to prove this point it was not necessary to ask for the condition that
P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})\varphi\land(\mathrm{-})
preserves finite joins in
P(X\times A)
, which was necessary for
\mathfrak{f}
to preserve finite joins in Proposition
5.9
: it is enough to ask
\mathfrak{g}
to preserve them.

(vii)

It follows trivially combining the previous properties.∎

A stronger result for Theorem
6.2
holds. Let again
P:\mathbb{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
be a primary doctrine; fix on object
X
in the base category, and an element
\varphi\in P(X)
. For any other primary doctrine
R:\mathbb{D}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
, define the category
\mathbf{PD}_{(X,\varphi)}(P,R)
whose objects are pairs of the kind
\big((G,\mathfrak{g}),c:\mathbf{t}_{\mathbb{D}}\to GX\big)
, where
(G,\mathfrak{g})\in\mathbf{PD}(P,R)
, such that
\top\leq R(c)\mathfrak{g}_{X}(\varphi)
in
R(\mathbf{t}_{\mathbb{D}})
, and whose arrows are 2-arrows preserving the constant, meaning
\theta:\big((G,\mathfrak{g}),c\big)\to\big((H,\mathfrak{h}),d\big)
is a 2-arrow
\theta:(G,\mathfrak{g})\to(H,\mathfrak{h})
in
\mathbf{PD}
such that
c\theta_{X}=d
. There is an obvious functor induced by precomposition with
(F_{X},\mathfrak{f})
, from
\mathbf{PD}(P_{(X,\varphi)},R)
to
\mathbf{PD}_{(X,\varphi)}(P,R)
: it maps any
\xi:(K,\mathfrak{k})\to(K^{\prime},\mathfrak{k}^{\prime})
into
\xi_{F_{X}}:\big((K,\mathfrak{k})\circ(F_{X},\mathfrak{f}),K(\OPRid_{X})\big)\to\big((K^{\prime},\mathfrak{k}^{\prime})\circ(F_{X},\mathfrak{f}),K^{\prime}(\OPRid_{X})\big)
. This is well defined on objects since
R(K(\OPRid_{X}))(\mathfrak{k}_{F_{X}X}\mathfrak{f}_{X}\varphi)=\mathfrak{k}_{\mathbf{t}}P_{(X,\varphi)}(\OPRid_{X})\mathfrak{f}_{X}\varphi=\top
, and well defined on arrows since
K(\OPRid_{X})\xi_{X}=K^{\prime}(\OPRid_{X})
by naturality.

Theorem 6.4
.

Let
P
be a primary doctrine. Given an object
X
in the base category and an element
\varphi\in P(X)
, the functor
\mathbf{PD}(P_{(X,\varphi)},R)\to\mathbf{PD}_{(X,\varphi)}(P,R)
induced by precomposition with
(F_{X},\mathfrak{f})
is an equivalence of categories for any primary doctrine
R
.

Proof.

The functor is essentially surjective following from Theorem
6.2
and faithfulness is trivial since
F_{X}
is the identity on objects. To show that the functor is full, take any 2-arrow
\theta:\big((K,\mathfrak{k})\circ(F_{X},\mathfrak{f}),K(\OPRid_{X})\big)\to\big((K^{\prime},\mathfrak{k}^{\prime})\circ(F_{X},\mathfrak{f}),K^{\prime}(\OPRid_{X})\big)
and prove that
\theta:(K,\mathfrak{k})\to(K^{\prime},\mathfrak{k}^{\prime})
is in
\mathbf{PD}(P_{(X,\varphi)},R)
. First of all, we check that it is a natural transformation
K\to K^{\prime}
: take any
f:A\rightsquigarrow B
in
\mathbb{C}_{X}
and break it as the composition of
\OPRid_{X\times A}:A\rightsquigarrow X\times A
and
f\langle\mathrm{pr}_{2},\mathrm{pr}_{3}\rangle=F_{X}(f):X\times A\rightsquigarrow B
; moreover observe that
\OPRid_{X\times A}
has as first projection the composition of the unique arrow
A\rightsquigarrow\mathbf{t}
and the constant
\OPRid_{X}:\mathbf{t}\rightsquigarrow X
, and as second projection the identity
\mathrm{pr}_{2}:A\rightsquigarrow A
—see the end of the proof of Theorem
6.2
. So the naturality diagram becomes:
