The lower square commutes since
\theta:KF_{X}\xrightarrow{\cdot}K^{\prime}F_{X}
by assumption, while the upper square commutes since
K(\OPRid_{X})\theta_{X}=K^{\prime}(\OPRid_{X})
. To conclude, we need for any
\mathbb{C}
-object
A
and any
\alpha\in P_{(X,\varphi)}(A)=P(X\times A)_{\downarrow P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})\varphi}
the inequality
\mathfrak{k}_{A}(\alpha)\leq R(\theta_{A})\mathfrak{k}^{\prime}_{A}(\alpha)
to hold. In particular
\alpha\in P(X\times A)
, so we can consider

\mathfrak{f}_{X\times A}\alpha=P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\varphi)\land P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{2},\mathrm{pr}_{3}\rangle)(\alpha)\in P_{(X,\varphi)}(X\times A)\subseteq P(X\times X\times A);

apply then naturality of
\mathfrak{k}
to
\OPRid_{X\times A}:A\rightsquigarrow X\times A
to observe that

R(\langle K(\OPRid_{X})!_{KA},\OPRid_{KA}\rangle)\mathfrak{k}_{X\times A}\mathfrak{f}_{X\times A}\alpha=\mathfrak{k}_{A}P_{(X,\varphi)}(\OPRid_{X\times A})\mathfrak{f}_{X\times A}\alpha\\
=\mathfrak{k}_{A}P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{2}\rangle)\big(P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\varphi)\land P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{2},\mathrm{pr}_{3}\rangle)(\alpha)\big)=\mathfrak{k}_{A}(P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\varphi)\land\alpha)=\mathfrak{k}_{A}(\alpha)

since
\alpha\leq P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\varphi)
. Moreover, since in particular
\theta:(K,\mathfrak{k})\circ(F_{X},\mathfrak{f})\to(K^{\prime},\mathfrak{k}^{\prime})\circ(F_{X},\mathfrak{f})
, we know that
\mathfrak{k}_{X\times A}\mathfrak{f}_{X\times A}(\alpha)\leq R(\theta_{X\times A})\mathfrak{k}^{\prime}_{X\times A}\mathfrak{f}_{X\times A}(\alpha)
. So we have:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{k}_{A}(\alpha)

\displaystyle=R(\langle K(\OPRid_{X})!_{KA},\OPRid_{KA}\rangle)\mathfrak{k}_{X\times A}\mathfrak{f}_{X\times A}\alpha

\displaystyle\leq R(\langle K(\OPRid_{X})!_{KA},\OPRid_{KA}\rangle)R(\theta_{X\times A})\mathfrak{k}^{\prime}_{X\times A}\mathfrak{f}_{X\times A}(\alpha)

\displaystyle=R(\theta_{A})R(\langle K^{\prime}(\OPRid_{X})!_{K^{\prime}A},\OPRid_{K^{\prime}A}\rangle)\mathfrak{k}^{\prime}_{X\times A}\mathfrak{f}_{X\times A}(\alpha)=R(\theta_{A})\mathfrak{k}^{\prime}_{A}(\alpha).\qed

The process studied above in Theorem
6.2
describes how to add a constant of sort
X
that verifies a formula
\varphi
in a universal way. Taking the particular case when
X=\mathbf{t}
is the terminal object, we are not adding any constant—the functor
\mathbf{t}\times\mathrm{-}:\mathbb{C}\to\mathbb{C}
is essentially the identity—, and we are just requiring
\varphi\in P(\mathbf{t})
to be true in the new doctrine—i.e. we are adding the axiom
\varphi
to the theory
P
—, in a universal way. In this case we write
P_{\varphi}:\mathbb{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
; for any given
\mathbb{C}
-arrow
f:A\to B
, we have
P_{\varphi}(f):P_{\varphi}(B)\to P_{\varphi}(A)
, computed as

P(f):P(B)_{\downarrow P(!_{B})\varphi}\to P(A)_{\downarrow P(!_{A})\varphi}.

The 1-arrow
P\to P_{\varphi}
becomes
(\OPRid_{\mathbb{C}},\mathfrak{f})
, where
\mathfrak{f}_{A}:P(A)\to P_{\varphi}(A)
maps an element
\alpha\in P(A)
to
P(!_{A})\varphi\land\alpha\in P_{\varphi}(A)
. All the additional properties of
P
described in Section
5
are clearly recovered by
P_{\varphi}
.

Corollary 6.5
.

Let
P:\mathbb{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
be a primary doctrine. Given an element
\varphi\in P(\mathbf{t})
, the 1-arrow
(\OPRid_{\mathbb{C}},\mathfrak{f}):P\to P_{\varphi}
is such that
\top\leq\mathfrak{f}_{\mathbf{t}}(\varphi)
in
P_{\varphi}(\mathbf{t})
, and it is universal with respect to this property, i.e. for any primary 1-arrow
(G,\mathfrak{g}):P\to R
, where
R:\mathbb{D}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
is a primary doctrine, such that
\top\leq\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbf{t}}(\varphi)
in
R(\mathbf{t}_{\mathbb{D}})
there exists a unique up to a unique natural isomorphism primary 1-arrow
(G^{\prime},\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}):P_{\varphi}\to R
such that
(G^{\prime},\mathfrak{g}^{\prime})\circ(\OPRid_{\mathbb{C}},\mathfrak{f})=(G,\mathfrak{g})
.

The category corresponding to
\mathbf{PD}_{(X,\varphi)}(P,R)
in Theorem
6.4
for some primary doctrine
R:\mathbb{D}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
in this case is called
\mathbf{PD}_{\varphi}(P,R)
: objects are morphisms
(G,\mathfrak{g})\in\mathbf{PD}(P,R)
such that
\top\leq\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbf{t}}(\varphi)
in
R(\mathbf{t}_{\mathbb{D}})
and arrows are 2-arrows of
\mathbf{PD}
. In particular
\mathbf{PD}_{\varphi}(P,R)
is a full subcategory of
\mathbf{PD}(P,R)
. Precomposition with
(\OPRid_{\mathbb{C}},\mathfrak{f})
is a functor from
\mathbf{PD}(P_{\varphi},R)
to
\mathbf{PD}(P,R)
, and has image in
\mathbf{PD}_{\varphi}(P,R)
: given
(K,\mathfrak{k}):P_{\varphi}\to R
, the composition
(K,\mathfrak{k})(\OPRid_{\mathbb{C}},\mathfrak{f})
is such that
\mathfrak{(kf)}_{\mathbf{t}}(\varphi)=\mathfrak{k}_{\mathbf{t}}\mathfrak{f}_{\mathbf{t}}(\varphi)=\mathfrak{k}_{\mathbf{t}}(\varphi)=\top
in
R(\mathbf{t}_{\mathbb{D}})
, since
\varphi
is the top element in
P_{\varphi}(\mathbf{t})
.

Corollary 6.6
.

Let
P
be a primary doctrine. Given an element
\varphi\in P(\mathbf{t})
, precomposition with
(\OPRid_{\mathbb{C}},\mathfrak{f})

\mathrm{-}\circ(\OPRid_{\mathbb{C}},\mathfrak{f}):\mathbf{PD}(P_{\varphi},R)\to\mathbf{PD}_{\varphi}(P,R)

is an equivalence of categories for any primary doctrine
R
.

Similarly, we can take the particular case when
\varphi=\top_{X}\in P(X)
is the top element, so we are not making any formula true—the natural transformation
\mathfrak{f}_{A}:P(A)\to P(X\times A)
represent the inclusion of formulae of sort
A
in the formulae of the same sort but in a language with a new constant—, and we are just adding a constant
\OPRid_{X}:\mathbf{t}\rightsquigarrow X
, in a universal way. In this case we write
P_{X}:\mathbb{C}_{X}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
; for any given
\mathbb{C}_{X}
-arrow
f:A\rightsquigarrow B
, we have
P_{X}(f):P_{X}(B)\to P_{X}(A)
computed as

P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},f\rangle):P(X\times B)\to P(X\times A).

The 1-arrow
P\to P_{X}
becomes
(F_{X},\mathfrak{f})
, where
\mathfrak{f}_{A}:P(A)\to P_{X}(A)
maps an element
\alpha\in P(A)
to
P(\mathrm{pr}_{2})(\alpha)\in P_{X}(A)
. All the additional properties of
P
described in Section
5
are clearly recovered by
P_{X}
. Observe that for this construction, the assumption that the starting doctrine
P
is primary is not needed: the reader can go through all proofs removing the primary assumption on the starting doctrine when
\varphi=\top_{X}
and every computation still works.

Corollary 6.7
.

Let
P:\mathbb{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
be a doctrine. Given an object
X
in the base category, the 1-arrow
(F_{X},\mathfrak{f}):P\to P_{X}
and the
\mathbb{C}_{X}
-arrow
\OPRid_{X}:\mathbf{t}_{\mathbb{C}_{X}}\rightsquigarrow X
are universal, i.e. for any 1-arrow
(G,\mathfrak{g}):P\to R
, where
R:\mathbb{D}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
is a doctrine, and any
\mathbb{D}
-arrow
c:\mathbf{t}_{\mathbb{D}}\to G(X)
there exists a unique up to a unique natural isomorphism 1-arrow
(G^{\prime},\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}):P_{X}\to R
such that
(G^{\prime},\mathfrak{g}^{\prime})\circ(F_{X},\mathfrak{f})=(G,\mathfrak{g})
and
G^{\prime}(\OPRid_{X})=c
.

The category corresponding to
\mathbf{PD}_{(X,\varphi)}(P,R)
in Theorem
6.4
can be defined for any doctrine
R:\mathbb{D}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
, and in this case is called
\mathbf{Dct}_{X}(P,R)
: objects are pairs
\big((G,\mathfrak{g}),c:\mathbf{t}\to GX\big)
where
(G,\mathfrak{g})\in\mathbf{Dct}(P,R)
and arrows are 2-arrows of
\mathbf{Dct}
preserving the constant. Precomposition with
(F_{X},\mathfrak{f})
induces a functor from
\mathbf{Dct}(P_{X},R)
to
\mathbf{Dct}_{X}(P,R)
: it maps any
\xi:(K,\mathfrak{k})\to(K^{\prime},\mathfrak{k}^{\prime})
into
\xi_{F_{X}}:\big((K,\mathfrak{k})\circ(F_{X},\mathfrak{f}),K(\OPRid_{X})\big)\to\big((K^{\prime},\mathfrak{k}^{\prime})\circ(F_{X},\mathfrak{f}),K^{\prime}(\OPRid_{X})\big)
. This is well defined on arrows since
K(\OPRid_{X})\xi_{X}=K^{\prime}(\OPRid_{X})
by naturality.

Corollary 6.8
.

Let
P
be a doctrine. Given an object
X
in the base category, the functor
\langle\mathrm{-}\circ(F_{X},\mathfrak{f}),\mathrm{-}(\OPRid_{X})\rangle:\mathbf{Dct}(P_{X},R)\to\mathbf{Dct}_{X}(P,R)
induced by precomposition with
(F_{X},\mathfrak{f})
is an equivalence of categories for any doctrine
R
.

Remark 6.9
.

We showed how to obtain from the universal 1-arrow
(F_{X},\mathfrak{f}_{(X,\varphi)}):P\to P_{(X,\varphi)}
for fixed object
X
and element
\varphi\in P(X)
, both universal 1-arrows
(F_{X},\mathfrak{f}_{X}):P\to P_{X}
in Corollary
6.7
for a fixed object
X
and
(\OPRid_{\mathbb{C}},\mathfrak{f}_{\varphi}):P\to P_{\varphi}
in Corollary
6.5
for a fixed element
\varphi
in
P(\mathbf{t})
as particular cases. Note that we wrote some subscripts to avoid confusion between the constructions. We now show that we can recover the first 1-arrow from the other two. To do so, take a primary doctrine
P:\mathbb{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
, fix an object
X
and an element
\varphi\in P(X)
. Apply the construction that adds a constant to obtain
(F_{X},\mathfrak{f}_{X}):P\to P_{X}
. Now consider the primary doctrine
P_{X}:\mathbb{C}_{X}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
and the element
\varphi
in the fiber over the terminal object
P_{X}(\mathbf{t})=P(X)
. Apply the construction that adds an axiom to obtain
(\OPRid_{\mathbb{C}_{X}},\mathfrak{f}_{\varphi}):P_{X}\to(P_{X})_{\varphi}
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}^{\mathrm{op}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{C}_{X}^{\mathrm{op}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{C}_{X}^{\mathrm{op}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{Pos}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{X}^{\mathrm{op}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\OPRid_{\mathbb{C}_{X}}^{\mathrm{op}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(P_{X})_{\varphi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{f}_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{f}_{\varphi}}

Compute for each object
A
, the poset
(P_{X})_{\varphi}(A)=P_{X}(A)_{\downarrow P_{X}(!_{X\times A})\varphi}
, where
!_{X\times A}:A\rightsquigarrow\mathbf{t}
is the unique
\mathbb{C}_{X}
-arrow from
A
to
\mathbf{t}
. The reindexing along this arrow is
P_{X}(!_{X\times A}):P_{X}(\mathbf{t})\to P_{X}(A)
, that maps
\varphi
to
P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})\varphi
, so
(P_{X})_{\varphi}(A)=P(X\times A)_{\downarrow P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})\varphi}
which is exactly how the fibers of
P_{(X,\varphi)}
are computed. Then compute reindexing in
(P_{X})_{\varphi}
: given
f:A\rightsquigarrow B
, we know that
(P_{X})_{\varphi}(f)
is defined as the restriction of
P_{X}(f)
, that is
P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},f\rangle)
, which is how reindexing are computed in
P_{(X,\varphi)}
. So the functor
(P_{X})_{\varphi}
is
P_{(X,\varphi)}
. Moreover, observe that the composition of the 1-arrows is
(\OPRid_{\mathbb{C}_{X}},\mathfrak{f}_{\varphi})(F_{X},\mathfrak{f}_{X})=(F_{X},\mathfrak{f}_{(X,\varphi)})
.

Remark 6.10
.

Using the description of the fibers of
P_{(X,\varphi)}
in
Remark

4.2
, it is worth noticing that the two constructions of adding a constant and adding an axiom have essentially the same shape on the base category and on the fibers. On the one hand, in the new base category, homomorphism between two objects
A
and
B
are old homomorphism between
X\times A
and
B
; more precisely
\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_{\mathbb{C}_{X}}(A,B)=\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_{\mathbb{C}}(X\times A,B)
. On the other hand, looking at a poset with finite meets as a category with finite products, we have for any pair
\alpha,\beta\in P(X\times A)
that
\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}([\alpha],[\beta])=\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}(P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\varphi)\land\alpha,\beta)
. Roughly speaking, in each fiber poset, adding a constant of sort
P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\varphi)
means to force the existence of a (necessarily unique) arrow from the terminal object to
P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\varphi)
, i.e. forcing
P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\varphi)
to become true, hence in our construction we are actually adding an axiom.

In the following, we apply separately the two constructions to a doctrine of well-formed formulae in some language
\mathcal{L}
and theory
\mathcal{T}
. At first, we apply the construction that adds a constant to a doctrine, and show that there is an isomorphism between this doctrine and the doctrine of well-formed formulae in the language with a new constant symbol. Then we apply the construction that adds an axiom to a doctrine, and show that there is an isomorphism between this doctrine and the doctrine of well-formed formulae where the theory has a new axiom.

Example 6.11
.

Let
\mathcal{L}
be a first-order language and
\mathcal{T}
be a theory. Consider the doctrine of well-formed formulae
\mathtt{LT}^{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathcal{T}}:\mathrm{\mathbb{C}tx}_{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
and the fixed object
(x)
in the base category
\mathrm{\mathbb{C}tx}_{\mathcal{L}}
. On the one hand, consider the 1-arrow
(F_{(x)},\mathfrak{f}):\mathtt{LT}^{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathcal{T}}\to(\mathtt{LT}^{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathcal{T}})_{(x)}
, where
(\mathtt{LT}^{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathcal{T}})_{(x)}:(\mathrm{\mathbb{C}tx}_{\mathcal{L}})_{(x)}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
. Arrows in
(\mathrm{\mathbb{C}tx}_{\mathcal{L}})_{(x)}
are of the form
\vec{t}(\,(x);\vec{z}\,):\vec{z}\rightsquigarrow\vec{y}
, and the fibers
(\mathtt{LT}^{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathcal{T}})_{(x)}(\vec{z})
are
\mathtt{LT}^{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathcal{T}}(\,(x);\vec{z}\,)
for any list of variables
\vec{z}
. On the other hand consider the doctrine
\mathtt{LT}^{\mathcal{L}\cup\{c\}}_{\mathcal{T}}:\mathrm{\mathbb{C}tx}_{\mathcal{L}\cup\{c\}}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
, where
c
is a constant symbol not appearing in
\mathcal{L}
. There is a trivial 1-arrow
(E,\mathfrak{e}):\mathtt{LT}^{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathcal{T}}\to\mathtt{LT}^{\mathcal{L}\cup\{c\}}_{\mathcal{T}}
: the functor
E
is defined by the inclusion of terms in the extended language, the natural transformation
\mathfrak{e}
is defined by the inclusion of formulae. The universal property of
(F_{(x)},\mathfrak{f})
defines a unique
(E^{\prime},\mathfrak{e}^{\prime}):(\mathtt{LT}^{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathcal{T}})_{(x)}\to\mathtt{LT}^{\mathcal{L}\cup\{c\}}_{\mathcal{T}}
such that
(E^{\prime},\mathfrak{e}^{\prime})(F_{(x)},\mathfrak{f})=(E,\mathfrak{e})
and such that
E^{\prime}\big(\OPRid_{(x)}:()\rightsquigarrow(x)\big)=\big(c:()\to(x)\big)
.
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{\mathbb{C}tx}_{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{op}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{\mathbb{C}tx}_{\mathcal{L}\cup\{c\}}^{\mathrm{op}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathrm{\mathbb{C}tx}_{\mathcal{L}})_{(x)}^{\mathrm{op}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{Pos}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{(x)}^{\mathrm{op}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathtt{LT}^{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathcal{T}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E^{\mathrm{op}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathtt{LT}^{\mathcal{L}\cup\{c\}}_{\mathcal{T}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathtt{LT}^{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathcal{T}})_{(x)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{op}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{e}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{e}}

The functor
E^{\prime}
maps an arrow
\vec{t}(\,(x);\vec{z}\,):\vec{z}\rightsquigarrow\vec{y}
to the term
\vec{t}(\,[c/x];\vec{z}\,):\vec{z}\to\vec{y}
in
\mathrm{\mathbb{C}tx}_{\mathcal{L}\cup\{c\}}
. For a given pair
\vec{t}(\,(x);\vec{z}\,),\vec{s}(\,(x);\vec{z}\,)
such that
\vec{t}(\,[c/x];\vec{z}\,)=\vec{s}(\,[c/x];\vec{z}\,)
, substitute again
[x/c]
and get
\vec{t}=\vec{s}
, so
E^{\prime}
is faithful. Then, for a given a term
u(\vec{z})
in the language
{\mathcal{L}\cup\{c\}}
, we can consider
c
as a variable and substitute each occurrence of
c
with
x
, to obtain a term
u^{\prime}(\,(x);\vec{z}\,)
obviously written in the language
\mathcal{L}
: in particular
E^{\prime}(u^{\prime})=u^{\prime}(\,[c/x];\vec{z}\,)=u(\vec{z})
, so
E^{\prime}
is full. Moreover, since
E^{\prime}
is the identity on objects,
E^{\prime}
is an isomorphism.

Concerning formulae, a component of the natural transformation
\mathfrak{e}^{\prime}_{(\vec{z})}
sends a formula
\alpha(\,(x);\vec{z}\,)
in
(\mathtt{LT}^{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathcal{T}})_{(x)}(\vec{z})=\mathtt{LT}^{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathcal{T}}(\,(x);\vec{z}\,)
to the formula
\alpha(\,[c/x];\vec{z}\,)\in\mathtt{LT}^{\mathcal{L}\cup\{c\}}_{\mathcal{T}}(\vec{z})
. A similar argument to the one that showed fullness of the functor
E^{\prime}
proves that
\mathfrak{e}^{\prime}
is a natural isomorphism.

To conclude, we can say that the doctrine
(\mathtt{LT}^{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathcal{T}})_{(x)}
is again a doctrine of well-formed formulae.

Example 6.12
.

Let
\mathcal{L}
be a first-order language and
\mathcal{T}
be a theory. Consider the doctrine of well-formed formulae
\mathtt{LT}^{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathcal{T}}:\mathrm{\mathbb{C}tx}_{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
and the fixed
\mathcal{L}
-sentence
\varphi\in\mathtt{LT}^{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathcal{T}}()
. On the one hand, consider the 1-arrow
(\OPRid_{\mathrm{\mathbb{C}tx}_{\mathcal{L}}},\mathfrak{f}):\mathtt{LT}^{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathcal{T}}\to(\mathtt{LT}^{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathcal{T}})_{\varphi}
, where
(\mathtt{LT}^{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathcal{T}})_{\varphi}:\mathrm{\mathbb{C}tx}_{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
. Its fibers
(\mathtt{LT}^{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathcal{T}})_{\varphi}(\vec{z})
are by definition
\mathtt{LT}^{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathcal{T}}(\vec{z})_{\downarrow\varphi}
for any list of variables
\vec{z}
. On the other hand consider the doctrine
\mathtt{LT}^{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathcal{T}\cup\{\varphi\}}:\mathrm{\mathbb{C}tx}_{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
. There is an obvious 1-arrow
(\OPRid_{\mathrm{\mathbb{C}tx}_{\mathcal{L}}},\mathfrak{e}):\mathtt{LT}^{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathcal{T}}\to\mathtt{LT}^{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathcal{T}\cup\{\varphi\}}
: the natural transformation
\mathfrak{e}
is defined by the quotient of formulae with respect to the extended theory, meaning that for each component
\vec{x}
it maps any
\mathcal{T}
-provable sequent
\alpha(\vec{x})\vdash_{\mathcal{T}}\beta(\vec{x})
into the
\mathcal{T}\cup\{\varphi\}
-provable sequent
\alpha(\vec{x})\vdash_{\mathcal{T}\cup\{\varphi\}}\beta(\vec{x})
. To use the universal property of
(\OPRid_{\mathrm{\mathbb{C}tx}_{\mathcal{L}}},\mathfrak{f})
, we need to check that
\mathfrak{e}_{()}
maps
\varphi\in\mathtt{LT}^{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathcal{T}}()
to the top element of
\mathtt{LT}^{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathcal{T}\cup\{\varphi\}}()
. However this is true since clearly
\top\vdash_{\mathcal{T}\cup\{\varphi\}}\varphi
. Consequently there exists a unique
(E^{\prime},\mathfrak{e}^{\prime}):(\mathtt{LT}^{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathcal{T}})_{\varphi}\to\mathtt{LT}^{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathcal{T}\cup\{\varphi\}}
such that
(E^{\prime},\mathfrak{e}^{\prime})(\OPRid_{\mathrm{\mathbb{C}tx}_{\mathcal{L}}},\mathfrak{f})=(\OPRid_{\mathrm{\mathbb{C}tx}_{\mathcal{L}}},\mathfrak{e})
.
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{\mathbb{C}tx}_{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{op}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{\mathbb{C}tx}_{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{op}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{\mathbb{C}tx}_{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{op}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{Pos}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\OPRid_{\mathrm{\mathbb{C}tx}_{\mathcal{L}}}^{\mathrm{op}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathtt{LT}^{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathcal{T}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\OPRid_{\mathrm{\mathbb{C}tx}_{\mathcal{L}}}^{\mathrm{op}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathtt{LT}^{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathcal{T}\cup\{\varphi\}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathtt{LT}^{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathcal{T}})_{\varphi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\OPRid_{\mathrm{\mathbb{C}tx}_{\mathcal{L}}}^{\mathrm{op}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{e}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{e}}

The functor
E^{\prime}
is the identity.

A component of the natural transformation
\mathfrak{e}^{\prime}_{\vec{x}}
sends a formula
\alpha(\vec{x})
in
(\mathtt{LT}^{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathcal{T}})_{\varphi}(\vec{x})=\mathtt{LT}^{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathcal{T}}(\vec{x})_{\downarrow\varphi}
to the formula
\alpha(\vec{x})\in\mathtt{LT}^{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathcal{T}\cup\{\varphi\}}(\vec{x})
. Define the inverse function: it maps
\beta(\vec{x})
to
\beta(\vec{x})\land\varphi
. This is well defined and monotone, since if we take
\alpha(\vec{x})\vdash_{\mathcal{T}\cup\{\varphi\}}\beta(\vec{x})
, it easily follows that
\alpha(\vec{x})\land\varphi\vdash_{\mathcal{T}}\beta(\vec{x})\land\varphi
. On the one hand, take
\alpha(\vec{x})
such that
\alpha(\vec{x})\vdash_{\mathcal{T}}\varphi
, apply
\mathfrak{e}^{\prime}_{\vec{x}}
to get
\alpha(\vec{x})
, and then send it to
\alpha(\vec{x})\land\varphi
, and observe that
\alpha(\vec{x})\land\varphi\dashv\vdash_{\mathcal{T}}\alpha(\vec{x})
using the initial assumption on
\alpha(\vec{x})
. Conversely, take
\beta(\vec{x})\in\mathtt{LT}^{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathcal{T}\cup\{\varphi\}}(\vec{x})
, send it to
\beta(\vec{x})\land\varphi
, and then apply
\mathfrak{e}^{\prime}_{\vec{x}}
to get
\beta(\vec{x})\land\varphi\in\mathtt{LT}^{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathcal{T}\cup\{\varphi\}}(\vec{x})
. Observe that
\beta(\vec{x})\land\varphi\dashv\vdash_{\mathcal{T}\cup\{\varphi\}}\beta(\vec{x})
. So
\mathfrak{e}^{\prime}
is indeed a natural isomorphism.

To conclude, we can say that the doctrine
(\mathtt{LT}^{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathcal{T}})_{\varphi}
is again a doctrine of well-formed formulae.

Going towards a conclusion, in the following example we prove that if we apply our construction to the subsets doctrine
\mathscr{P}
trying to force the existence of an arrow in the empty set, the resulting doctrine collapses to a trivial one, meaning that all of its fibers are singletons.

Example 6.13
.

Consider the doctrine
\mathscr{P}:\textnormal{Set}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
and do the construction with respect to the object
\emptyset
.
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textnormal{Set}^{\text{op}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textnormal{Set}_{\emptyset}}^{\text{op}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{Pos}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{\emptyset}}^{\text{op}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{P}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{P}_{\emptyset}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}

In the category
\textnormal{Set}_{\emptyset}
all objects are isomorphic: indeed, for any pair of sets
A,B
, there exists exactly one homomorphism
A\rightsquigarrow B
corresponding to the inclusion
\emptyset\times A\to B
of the empty set in
B
. The fiber over a set
A
is
\mathscr{P}_{\emptyset}(A)=\mathscr{P}(\emptyset\times A)=\mathscr{P}(\emptyset)=\{\emptyset\}
, hence every fiber is trivial. In particular, the natural transformation
\mathfrak{f}
is the constant function on every component.

A similar problem arises if we apply our construction again to the subset doctrine
\mathscr{P}
trying to force the empty set
\emptyset\in\mathscr{P}(\{*\})
as a new axiom. While the base category
Set
is the same after the construction, the fibers all collapse as in
Example

6.13
.

Example 6.14
.

Consider the doctrine
\mathscr{P}:\textnormal{Set}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
and do the construction with respect to the element
\emptyset\in\mathscr{P}(\{*\})
.
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textnormal{Set}^{\text{op}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textnormal{Set}}^{\text{op}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{Pos}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\OPRid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{P}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{P}_{\emptyset\subseteq\{*\}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}

Note that we wrote
\mathscr{P}_{\emptyset\subseteq\{*\}}
to remark the fact that here the empty set is an element in the fiber over the terminal object of
Set
, and not an object in the base cateogry as it was in
Example

6.13
.
The fiber over a set
A
is
\mathscr{P}_{\emptyset\subseteq\{*\}}(A)=\mathscr{P}(A)_{\downarrow\emptyset}=\{S\subseteq A\mid S\subseteq\emptyset\}=\{\emptyset\}
, hence every fiber is trivial. Also in this case, the natural transformation
\mathfrak{f}
is the constant function on every component.

Definition 6.15
.

Let
P:\mathbb{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
and
R:\mathbb{D}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
be two doctrines and let
(F,\mathfrak{f}):P\to R
be a model of
P
in
R
. The model
(F,\mathfrak{f})
is
conservative
if every component of
\mathfrak{f}
is full as a functor between posets.

The idea is that the model
(F_{X},\mathfrak{f}):P\to P_{(X,\varphi)}
is conservative if, for two given formulae
\alpha,\beta
in a fiber of the doctrine
P
—in the old language—, if
\mathfrak{f}_{A}\alpha\leq\mathfrak{f}_{A}\beta
—the interpretation of
\alpha
proves the interpretation of
\beta
in the new theory of the extended language—, then
\alpha\leq\beta
in
P(A)
—
\alpha
was already proving
\beta
in the old theory.
The examples above show that in general the morphism
(F_{X},\mathfrak{f}):P\to P_{(X,\varphi)}
is not conservative. However, if the doctrine
P
is existential, we have the following characterization.

Proposition 6.16
.

Let
P:\mathbb{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
be an existential doctrine and
P_{(X,\varphi)}
be the Kleisli object of the comonad
(P,(X\times\mathrm{-},\mathfrak{f}),\gamma,\varepsilon)
defined by the pair
X\in\mathbb{C}
and
\varphi\in P(X)
. The model
(F_{X},\mathfrak{f}):P\to P_{(X,\varphi)}
is conservative if and only if
\top_{\mathbf{t}}\leq\exists^{X}_{\mathbf{t}}\varphi
in
P(\mathbf{t})
.

Proof.

First of all, suppose that for every object
A
and for every pair
\alpha,\beta\in P(A)
, if
\mathfrak{f}_{A}\alpha\leq\mathfrak{f}_{A}\beta
then
\alpha\leq\beta
. Consider the unit of the adjunction
\varphi\leq P(!_{X})\exists^{X}_{\mathbf{t}}\varphi
in
P(X)
. However, apply
\mathfrak{f}_{\mathbf{t}}:P(\mathbf{t})\to P_{(X,\varphi)}(\mathbf{t})=P(X)_{\downarrow\varphi}
to both
\top_{\mathbf{t}}
and
\exists^{X}_{\mathbf{t}}\varphi
: we get

\displaystyle\mathfrak{f}_{\mathbf{t}}(\top_{\mathbf{t}})

\displaystyle=P(!_{X})(\top_{\mathbf{t}})\land\varphi=\varphi

\displaystyle\mathfrak{f}_{\mathbf{t}}(\exists^{X}_{\mathbf{t}}\varphi)

\displaystyle=P(!_{X})(\exists^{X}_{\mathbf{t}}\varphi)\land\varphi.

So from
\mathfrak{f}_{\mathbf{t}}\top\leq\mathfrak{f}_{\mathbf{t}}\exists^{X}_{\mathbf{t}}\varphi
, by fullness we get
\top_{\mathbf{t}}\leq\exists^{X}_{\mathbf{t}}\varphi
.

Conversely, suppose that
\top_{\mathbf{t}}\leq\exists^{X}_{\mathbf{t}}\varphi
in
P(\mathbf{t})
and
\mathfrak{f}_{A}(\alpha)\leq\mathfrak{f}_{A}(\beta)
in
P_{(X,\varphi)}(A)
, for some pair
\alpha,\beta\in P(A)
, or equivalently
P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})\varphi\land P(\mathrm{pr}_{2})\alpha\leq P(\mathrm{pr}_{2})\beta
in
P(X\times A)
. We switch the coordinates and work in
P(A\times X)
: we get
P(\mathrm{pr}_{2})\varphi\land P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})\alpha\leq P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})\beta
. Then use the adjunction
\exists^{X}_{A}\dashv P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})
to obtain
\exists^{X}_{A}\big(P(\mathrm{pr}_{2})\varphi\land P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})\alpha\big)\leq\beta
in
P(A)
. Using Frobenius reciprocity and Beck-Chevalley condition we then get
\exists^{X}_{A}\big(P(\mathrm{pr}_{2})\varphi\land P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})\alpha\big)=\exists^{X}_{A}P(\mathrm{pr}_{2})\varphi\land\alpha=P(!_{A}){\exists^{X}_{\mathbf{t}}\varphi}\land\alpha=P(!_{A})\top_{\mathbf{t}}\land\alpha=\alpha
, where we used the assumption in the third equality. Hence
\alpha\leq\beta
as claimed.∎

Example 6.17
.

Consider the doctrine
\mathscr{P}:\textnormal{Set}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
and do the construction with respect to the object
X
.
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textnormal{Set}^{\text{op}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textnormal{Set}_{X}}^{\text{op}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{Pos}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{X}}^{\text{op}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{P}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{P}_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}

In the category
\textnormal{Set}_{X}
objects are sets, and an arrow
A\rightsquigarrow B
is a function
X\times A\to B
. The fiber over a set
A
is
\mathscr{P}_{X}(A)=\mathscr{P}(X\times A)=\mathscr{P}(X\times A)
, and the reindexing along
f:A\rightsquigarrow B
maps
S\subseteq X\times B
to
\{(x,a)\in X\times A\mid(x,f(x,a))\in S\}
. The natural transformation
\mathfrak{f}
at an object
A\in\textnormal{Set}
maps
S\subseteq A
to
X\times S\subseteq X\times A
.

Example 6.18
.

Consider the doctrine
\mathscr{P}:\textnormal{Set}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
and do the construction with respect to the object
X
and the element
Y\in\mathscr{P}(X)
.
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textnormal{Set}^{\text{op}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textnormal{Set}_{X}}^{\text{op}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{Pos}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{X}}^{\text{op}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{P}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{P}_{(X,Y)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}

In the category
\textnormal{Set}_{X}
is defined as in
Example

6.17
. The fiber over a set
A
is

\mathscr{P}_{(X,Y)}(A)=\mathscr{P}(X\times A)_{\downarrow\mathrm{pr}_{1}^{-1}[Y]}=\{S\subseteq X\times A\mid S\subseteq Y\times A\}=\mathscr{P}(Y\times A),

and the reindexing along
f:A\rightsquigarrow B
maps
S\subseteq Y\times B
to
\{(x,a)\in Y\times A\mid(x,f(x,a))\in S\}
. The natural transformation
\mathfrak{f}
at an object
A\in\textnormal{Set}
maps
S\subseteq A
to
Y\times S=(X\times S)\cap(Y\times A)\subseteq Y\times A
.

Remark 6.19
.

Comparing
Examples

6.17
and
6.18
above, we can observe that for a given pair of sets
X,Y
with
Y\subseteq X
, the construction that adds a constant of sort
Y
to the subset doctrine as in
Example

6.17
, and the construction that adds a constant of sort
X
and then the axiom
Y
evaluated in the new constant as in
Example

6.18
, have the same fibers: in both cases, the fiber over a set
A
is
\mathscr{P}(Y\times A)
. The only difference between the two constructions is in the arrows of the base categories. However, by the universal property of
\mathscr{P}\to\mathscr{P}_{(X,Y)}
, we have the following factorisation of the doctrine homomorphism
\mathscr{P}\to\mathscr{P}_{Y}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textnormal{Set}^{\mathrm{op}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textnormal{Set}_{Y}^{\mathrm{op}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textnormal{Set}_{X}^{\mathrm{op}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{Pos}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{X}^{\mathrm{op}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{P}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{Y}^{\mathrm{op}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{P}_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{P}_{(X,Y)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{f}_{X,Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{f}_{Y}}

(6.2)

Indeed, consider the arrow
\iota:\mathbf{t}\rightsquigarrow X
in
\textnormal{Set}_{Y}
corresponding to the inclusion
\iota:Y\to X
in
Set
. The arrow
\iota
is such that
\top\leq\mathscr{P}_{Y}(\iota)(\mathfrak{f}_{Y})_{X}(Y)
in
\mathscr{P}_{Y}(\{*\})
, since
Y
is the top element of
\mathscr{P}_{Y}(\{*\})
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{P}(X)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{P}_{Y}(X)=\mathscr{P}(Y\times X)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{P}_{Y}(\{*\})=\mathscr{P}(Y)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y\times Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\{y\in Y\mid(y,y)\in Y\times Y\}=Y.}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathfrak{f}_{Y})_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\OPRid_{Y},\iota\rangle^{-1}}

Thus, by
Theorem

6.2
, we have the factorisation in (
6.2
). The functor
\textnormal{Set}_{X}\to\textnormal{Set}_{Y}
between the base categories is the identity on objects, and maps a function
g:X\times A\to B
to its restriction
g(\iota\times\OPRid_{A}):Y\times A\to B
; the natural transformation is the identity on each fiber.

Example 6.20
.

We provide an interpretation of the universal property in
Corollary

6.5
. Let
\mathcal{L}=\{\cdot,e,(\mathrm{-})^{-1}\}
be the language of groups, let
\mathcal{T}
be the theory of groups and let
\varphi\in\mathtt{LT}^{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathcal{T}}()
be the commutativity axiom
\forall x\forall y(x\cdot y=y\cdot x)
. Set-teoretic models for the theory
\mathcal{T}
, i.e. groups, are in a one-to-one correspondence with doctrine homomorphisms
\mathtt{LT}^{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathcal{T}}\to\mathscr{P}
preserving boolean, existential and elementary structure
[
MR13b
]
. Similarly, abelian groups are in a one-to-one correspondence with boolean existential elementary doctrine homomorphisms
\mathtt{LT}^{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathcal{T}\cup\{\varphi\}}\to\mathscr{P}
. As we saw in
Example

6.12
, the doctrine
\mathtt{LT}^{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathcal{T}\cup\{\varphi\}}
is obtained from the doctrine
\mathtt{LT}^{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathcal{T}}
by adding the axiom
\varphi
. So let
H
be a group and let
(G,\mathfrak{g}):\mathtt{LT}^{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathcal{T}}\to\mathscr{P}
be the correspondent doctrine homomorphism. The group
H
is abelian if and only if
(G,\mathfrak{g})
factors through the extension morphism
\mathtt{LT}^{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathcal{T}}\to(\mathtt{LT}^{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathcal{T}})_{\varphi}
. This is a way to read what is stated in
Corollary

6.5
: roughly speaking, given a primary doctrine
P
and an element
\varphi\in P(\mathbf{t})
, a model
(G,\mathfrak{g}):P\to R
satisfies
\varphi
if and only if
\top\leq\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbf{t}}(\varphi)
in
R(\mathbf{t})
if and only if the model
(G,\mathfrak{g})
factors through
P\to P_{\varphi}
.
